#10326. This tiny gamepad with dual analog thumbsticks pushes the limits of portable controllers

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Larger hands may struggle with the Atom Plus, but it squeezes a lot of functionality into an extremely tiny controller.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

As much as I appreciate the ingenious design of the Nintendo Switch’s Joy-Con, my family and I can’t stand using them as standalone gamepads. So when we travel with the Switch 2, we always bring at least one full-size gamepad, plus a couple of 8BitDo’s Zero 2 controllers for games that don’t use analog thumbsticks. I’ve spent years hunting for an alternative that pairs the size of the Zero 2 with as much of the functionality of a larger controller as I can get, and CRKD’s new Atom Plus is nearly exactly that. But if you have larger hands like I do, you may find the gamepad’s focus on portability a struggle.

Measuring around 3.5 inches wide, the $29.99 Atom Plus is about a half-inch smaller than the 8BitDo Zero 2 but squeezes in considerably more features and functionality. The most obvious is a pair of symmetrical analog thumbsticks with anti-drift tunneling magnetoresistance technology. You also get shoulder buttons and triggers, rumble feedback, a dedicated home button, motion controls, and customization through a mobile app.

Like many third-party controllers, the Atom Plus works with multiple platforms, including the Switch and Switch 2, PCs, Android, iOS, and various smart TVs. And just like other gamepads, pairing to different devices requires putting the Atom Plus into one of several Bluetooth modes by holding down a combination of buttons at power-up, which in this case is the home button plus a specific direction on the D-pad. It’s easy to do, but never easy to remember: I frequently needed to grab the manual or quickly Google which direction I need to press when switching devices. The Atom Plus already features a bunch of technical info etched into the back of the controller, so I’d love to see these important pairing instructions included there, too. (And that goes for all multiplatform gamepads.)

I primarily tested the Atom Plus with the Nintendo Switch 2 since I mostly plan to use the controller as a travel accessory. The thumbsticks are about the same size as those on the original Switch Joy-Con, which means they’re smaller than the Switch 2’s. And while I prefer an asymmetrical layout, the Atom Plus’ sticks are very good, with a little more tension than Nintendo’s that I find actually helps with precision movements given their limited travel.

While the overall build quality feels excellent, the Atom Plus’ tiny footprint creates some compromises and challenges. The front action buttons and D-pad feel nice and squishy, but the gamepad’s controls are a little crowded. When pressing down or right on the D-pad, my thumb regularly bumps the left thumbstick. It doesn’t hinder gameplay, but I do find it an annoying distraction.

The shoulder buttons use digital microswitches, as do the triggers, which means the latter can’t be used for racing games that support analog acceleration controls. I like that the two sets of buttons on the top edge sit at slightly different heights, but I would have preferred a little more differentiation since I’ve occasionally accidentally pressed the triggers when I meant to hit the shoulder buttons.

I was impressed with the Atom Plus’ customizability, however. When paired to an accompanying mobile app, you can check the controller’s remaining charge, turn off rumble feedback, see its unique product number (by tapping your phone to an NFC chip inside the controller), and remap any of its controls. That includes the pair of dual-function shortcut levers that replicate the Switch’s plus and minus buttons and screenshot shortcut.

Those levers move up and down and cleverly reduce the number of buttons crowding the front of the Atom Plus, but they feel a little too small, which is more or less the general theme. It’s a tiny gamepad that’s great for travel or stashing in your everyday carry. But, unsurprisingly, it probably won’t find its way into your regular gaming rotation. It’s assumed that players will be willing to settle for the compromise of occasionally contorting their fingers in weird, new ways for one of the smallest fully featured controllers on the market.

I don’t think a dual-stick controller could get any smaller than the Atom Plus without making too many ergonomic compromises. So while I applaud CRKD for the careful balancing act behind the Atom Plus’ design, and would certainly recommend it for those wanting a very capable controller that’s also cheap and portable, I’m now curious how it compares to Microsoft’s recently announced Xbox Nano controller that’s “surprisingly comfortable,” according to The Verge’s Tom Warren. It’s $20 more expensive than CRKD’s and noticeably larger, but it adds analog triggers, a more spacious design, and stubby grips making it easier to hold for those of us with beefy mitts.

#10324. Magic: The Gathering is getting a Netflix animated series — for real this time

Planeswalker is set to debut on Netflix in 2027.

Planeswalker is set to debut on Netflix in 2027.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

“It’s really happening. I promise. For real.” Those are the first words from Roy Graham, head of story for Magic: The Gathering, in a video posted yesterday announcing Planeswalker, the animated series heading to Netflix in 2027.

An animated Magic: The Gathering series was first teased back in 2019 with Joe and Anthony Russo at the helm. But that fell apart. In 2024, Netflix announced it still planned to make a series based on the game, but it decided to rework it from the ground up.

Now fans are finally getting their first glimpse of the show, which will be called Planeswalker. According to the sneak peek, because each plane exists in its own pocket universe, it will have a unique design, animation, and filmmaking style.

According to a synopsis of the show shared with IGN, Planeswalker will focus on Chandra Nalaar, a “volatile pyromancer hunted for her gifts, and haunted by her past.” Storyline details beyond that are vague, other than mentioning an “insidious force” that Chandra and other Planeswalkers will team up to confront “to prevent a cataclysmic war that threatens to consume their very existence.”

#10322. Nvidia claims its Vera CPU can gain up to 39% performance boost via software optimization at SC26 — architectural fine-tuning is needed to fully unleash custom Olympus cores

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Vera can speed up old code out of the box, but there's more performance to unlock

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia's Vera CPU, designed as both a standalone data-center part and a host for Nvidia's Vera Rubin NVL72 servers, offers impressive performance thanks to substantially higher execution resources per core than its predecessor, Grace. However, Nvidia admits at Supercomputing 2026 (SC26) that existing software cannot always take full advantage of Vera's resources without adaptation for the architecture.

The company shared that architecture-specific software optimizations can boost Vera's performance by nearly 39% in certain computationally intensive workloads. But despite offering unbeatable single-thread performance, the CPU is not positioned for general-purpose workloads.

Get the full Tom's Hardware experience on your device with our brand-new app. Bookmark articles, use our smart search feature, personalize your feed, and get push notifications so you never miss a beat.

Unlike Grace, which relies on off-the-shelf Neoverse V2 CPU cores developed by Arm, Vera uses Nvidia's proprietary Olympus cores specifically designed to maximize per-core performance as well as to ensure high aggregate throughput. To that end, Nvidia equipped Olympus with a wide front end that features a 10-wide decoder, a neural branch predictor, a deep out-of-order execution engine, six vector execution units, four load pipelines, two store pipelines, all backed with large private caches and advanced prefetch mechanisms.

In fact, Nvidia deliberately opted for 88 high-performance general-purpose cores with support for its proprietary spatial multithreading technology (176 threads in total) rather than maximizing the number of cores per socket, as the maximum possible single-thread performance was one of the key design goals for Vera.

"CPU design is really just a game of trade-offs," said Hannah Coutand, who leads Vera CPU marketing at Nvidia, in an interview with Tom's Hardware Premium. "You could maximize for a lot of cores, but literally when you maximize the die for lots of cores, you do not actually have space to maximize for IPC, like instructions per clock."

Although Nvidia primarily positions Vera as a CPU for agentic AI and other AI-related workloads, the company originally conceived its Olympus microarchitecture with data processing applications in mind. As it turned out, the key attributes of Vera — high per-core performance, high memory bandwidth, and low memory access latency — are precisely what agentic AI workloads need as well, according to Coutand.

"We are very clear in our strategy with Vera and our positioning with Vera that it is not a general-purpose CPU," Coutand said. " If you want a web server for your cloud data center, there are probably other choices that are better suited for that than Vera. [Vera is made for] workloads […] that require that high per-core speed, high memory bandwidth, low latency. […] It has great fungibility for lots of workloads as long as those workloads value the things that Vera is good for."

To assess the computational capabilities of Nvidia's custom Olympus microarchitecture and its advantages over Neoverse V2, Nvidia's researchers used a double-precision general matrix multiplication (DGEMM) workload based on an assembly kernel from OpenBLAS, an open-source linear algebra library. While OpenBLAS features a kernel tailored for the Arm Neoverse V2 cores that power Nvidia's Grace processor, it lacks one specifically designed for Olympus.

To examine the efficiency of the two microarchitectures, the researchers used a standalone benchmark called miniKernelGEMM with a 128 × 128 matrix multiplication problem that fits into the L1 cache of both processors.

The Grace CPU achieved 4.51 instructions per clock (IPC) and 13.03 FP64 operations per cycle with the Neoverse V2 kernel, whereas Vera executed 5.43 instructions per clock and 15.69 FP64 operations per cycle with the very same code, an improvement of approximately 20%.

However, when the researchers used a kernel specifically optimized for Olympus, Vera’s performance increased to 7.53 instructions per clock and 21.75 FP64 operations per cycle. As a result, Vera outperformed its predecessor by nearly 67% on a per-core, per-clock basis in this particular workload.

Grace's Neoverse V2 microarchitecture has four 128-bit SIMD pipelines that can perform a total of 16 FP64 operations per clock, whereas Nvidia's Olympus has six such pipelines that can execute up to 24 FP64 operations per cycle, a 50% improvement.

But where the Neoverse V2-optimized kernel uses 81.4% of Grace's peak FP64 throughput, it uses only about 66.5% of Vera's computational capabilities. With changes to matrix tile organization, register allocation, and instruction scheduling that better suit the Olympus microarchitecture, the researchers managed to utilize 90.6% of its peak FP64 throughput, according to Nvidia's tutorial published at SC26.

While software developed and optimized for existing Arm-based data center processors can still benefit from the capabilities of Nvidia's Olympus cores without any specific optimizations, the company's SC26 benchmark results demonstrate that at least some applications will need to be fine-tuned for the new microarchitecture to take full advantage of its execution resources. Of course, it remains to be seen how much performance can be gained through Olympus-specific optimizations in workloads other than matrix multiplication.

As Nvidia expands beyond CPUs that primarily serve as companions to its data center GPUs and chases the standalone server CPU market, the company will have to ensure that performance-critical software takes advantage of the architectural peculiarities of its processors, much like Intel and AMD have done for decades.

But the company is already deeply experienced with partner and ecosystem development for platforms like CUDA, so as Vera is more widely adopted, that experience could prove useful in spurring optimizations for these CPUs going forward.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10320. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

Upgrading a PC right now may be a non-starter, but you can still improve someone's overall desk experience without spending a lot.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It’s a tough time for PC gamers and enthusiasts. Graphics cards are scarce, RAM and storage are prohibitively expensive, and the PC port of GTA VI isn’t expected until sometime in 2027 at the earliest. But core components aside, there are plenty of other ways to improve someone's PC PC-related experience, and upgrading their desk space is a good place to start looking. We’ve already covered key peripherals, like keyboards and gaming accessories, so below we’ll be focusing on desk devices that let you connect more devices, stay more organized, improve comfort, or just keep things cleaner. From some of the best desk-friendly PC speakers I’ve tested to tactile media and software controls, and a monitor riser that integrates a USB hub and charging, there’s something here to improve everyone’s desk experience. I own most of these devices, or similar previous-gen versions that are no longer available, and can attest to their usefulness. Some live on my desk at the office, while others are better suited for home PC setups, but they all make for a better experience when I'm at my desk.

These unique wrist and elbow rests are designed to hang over the edge of your desk, making them great for those who lean on their elbows, or just for a cramped desk setup. Available in multiple colors and sizes, the 11.8-inch version I have is wide enough that I only need to use one for my compact keyboard at work. So I use the other one at home for my gaming setup.

If your phone has magnetic wireless Qi charging, there's no nicer, more convenient way to charge it than a stand like this, especially when there's additional power and ports for other devices. This Anker model has a built-in retractable cable that can handle up to 60W, so you can charge a non-gaming laptop with this stand at the same time as your phone. I've used a similar charging stand for years, and it helps keep things clean while making charging a second device as simple as grabbing the pull-out cable.

With 11 ports and up to 10 Gbps speed, a solid aluminum shell, and buttons so you can cut power to individual ports, this USB hub is a great addition to a desk without enough ports. It has external power for better stability, and even a 20W USB-C PD port on the end for charging phones or peripherals.

Just plug this simple device into a USB port, and you can control the volume and select tracks without reaching for your keyboard or mouse. It supports recent Windows versions, as well as Mac, without the need for a driver.

The Stream Deck Mini features six configurable buttons for launching software or controlling devices. It's compatible with the full suite of Stream Deck tools from Elgato. There are cheaper alternatives, but the ecosystem (supporting software as well as lights and smart devices) makes these devices more versatile than the competition.

This quirky device combines an under-desk headphone hook and a 20W PD USB charger with one USB-C and two USB-A ports. It brings a bit of extra convenience and tidiness to your desk setup. It comes in four colors and I've been using one under my standing desk since 2023.

These speakers are some of the best desk-friendly options we've tested, with great sound, lots of connection options, and excellent build quality. They're a great audio upgrade to most desk setups, and I have a pair that I bought in 2012 that still look and sound as good as they did when they were new.

We've all dealt with a crumb-covered desk or a dusty PC at some point. This all-in-one desk vacuum and portable air blower can tackle both those issues, and much more. Connect the included accessories to one end for blowing, and the other for suction. It's lived near my desk for years, and my main complaint is that Hoto doesn't ship with a wall mount or a good way to hold its many accessories. I solved that issue with my 3D printer.

This compact 1920 x 720 IPS monitor supports touch input and a 75 Hz refresh. I wouldn't recommend actually playing games on it, but it's a great second screen for game or work chats (or whatever else you want to keep a close eye on while doing other things). Its 24:9 aspect ratio (and a built-in kickstand) make it a good fit under your primary desktop monitor. It also costs less than competing name-brand alternatives.

You can think of this monitor stand as a sort of all-in-one desk upgrade. It packs a USB hub, 15W Qi charging, a 20W PD charging hub, built-in storage drawers, and a metal frame that has space for up to two 32-inch monitors. That's a lot of functionality in one device. I've owned a previous version of this stand since 2020. In fact, it's currently holding the monitor I'm looking at as I type this, and I've found it a good fit for my cramped office desk, since it helps keeps things tidy.

After a rough start with the Mattel Aquarius as a child, Matt built his first PC in the late 1990s and ventured into mild PC modding in the early 2000s. He’s spent the last 15 years covering emerging technology for Smithsonian, Popular Science, and Consumer Reports, while testing components and PCs for Computer Shopper, PCMag and Digital Trends.

#10315. Jensen’s exit could be Google’s TPU moment

Good morning. Today I’m rewatching Tenet to better picture Robert Pattinson in his new role, sighing over the inevitability of delivery drones triggering PTSD symptoms, and laugh-cringing at some of these Bit.ly link hijack stories from the weekend. Plus, prayers to anyone who had to find a last-minute hotel room during last week’s flying nightmare.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

In the immediate fallout of Jensen Huang’s humiliating resignation from Nvidia, coverage will focus on how the company plans to continue without its beloved former CEO, the world’s biggest champion of open-source AI and the proliferation of GPUs across borders. But in the long term, there’s an argument to be made that Google will be one of the largest winners of the shake-up, with a market opening up to competition outside of Nvidia’s CUDA moat.

While Colette Kress, the interim CEO, is unlikely to make any drastic changes to the company’s roadmap, the reality is that Nvidia’s competitors in the semiconductor space have desperately been working to unseat the market leader, and without Jensen at the helm, customers may be more likely to pursue alternatives.

For years, Google has offered its TPUs, or Tensor Processing Units, as a solution for AI training and inference, but they’ve been largely locked to its Google Cloud infrastructure. That’s changing now that the company is opening up sales to third parties and has announced it will bring TPU capacity to Oracle’s cloud beginning in 2027.

Google’s latest generation of TPUs is specialized; here’s how the company describes the lineup in its recent announcement post:

While flexible across workloads, together the four systems in our eighth-generation TPUs give customers a purpose-built accelerator for every stage of the agentic lifecycle. TPU 8t for scale-up training, TPU 8i for low-latency reasoning (inference), TPU 8tx for confidential computing, and TPU 8i Max for massive context windows and the highest compute density per rack.

Google isn’t comparing its new TPUs directly to Nvidia’s offerings, and clock-for-clock, even the most powerful TPU 8i Max doesn’t touch Vera Rubin for sheer performance. But this generation should be much more competitive for a few reasons: it’s more power efficient in certain configurations, and Google made it easier to translate coding workflows optimized for CUDA to work with its TPUs.

Back in October, Google open-sourced TorchTPU, which it co-developed with Meta’s PyTorch team, to let developers use familiar PyTorch workflows on TPUs with minimal code changes. That won’t make every custom CUDA operation portable or eliminate performance tuning, but reducing the effort required to try an alternative may change the buying decision. Customers don’t need to abandon Nvidia outright for Google to increase its TPU market share, or for Nvidia to face more pressure on price.

Alongside Broadcom and MediaTek, Google is taking on the Nvidia behemoth, and in the short term, it will almost certainly see a large upside in sentiment. But in the longer term, with scaling pressure lowering margins for Nvidia, Google’s specialized and more efficient TPUs may be a primary beneficiary of this sea change. They could also offset a slowdown in Search as Google’s shift to Cloud continues apace.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the event, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Over the past few days, the one-two punch of Microsoft’s 365/Exchange outage and the FAA’s ground stop shows just how vulnerable everyone is to the effects of seemingly brittle tech infrastructure.

The FAA describes a flood of connection attempts that destabilized its telecommunications network, affecting aircraft communications and radar feeds. Investigators identified sites in Arkansas and Texas that were undergoing router and wire replacements, but even days after the stop was lifted, there’s still no clear underlying cause.

Meanwhile, the Microsoft 365 outage lasted about 30 hours, with service restored to the remaining customers and regions late Friday. Microsoft says that a “latent defect” introduced into its network code in a July update wasn’t triggered until a few days ago, when high loads led to cascading failures across the company’s servers worldwide.

While neither AI nor cyberattacks were to blame, individually and together the two outages add urgency to getting companies to shore up their defenses and redundancies in a world where we’re overly reliant on digital systems.

#10314. Hacker behind 2022 GTA VI leaks pleads guilty

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Arion Kurtaj leaked 90 videos of GTA VI years before its release.

Arion Kurtaj leaked 90 videos of GTA VI years before its release.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Arion Kurtaj, a member of the Lapsus$ cybercrime group who leaked 90 videos of Grand Theft Auto VI in 2022 and hacked other big companies, pleaded guilty on Monday, according to the UK’s Crown Prosecution Service (CPS).

Kurtaj, who is now 21, hacked BT/EE sometime between August and November 2021, and starting in early November of that year, the company “noted a marked increase in unauthorised SIM swaps of their customers mobile phones.” He was charged for those offenses and moved to a hotel for his “own protection” in September 2022, but “breached his bail conditions to carry out further hacks on major companies using a mobile phone, the hotel TV’s Wi-Fi and a firestick,” including hacks on Rockstar Games, Uber, and Revolut, the CPS says.

He was sentenced to life in a secure hospital in December 2023 because of his autism, but in January, Kurtaj was deemed fit for trial and moved to prison, the BBC reports.

Kurtaj is set to be sentenced on November 23rd. GTA VI will be released on November 19th.

#10312. Google’s new ‘Fast Switch’ feature leaks, lets you use one number on two Android phones

Similar to a recent iPhone feature, Google appears to be working on “Fast Switch” as a way to share one SIM and phone number on two Android phones at once, and it sounds pretty cool.

Through the SIM Manager app on Android, the folks over at Android Authority noticed strings that tell a pretty clear story.

A new feature called “Android Fast Switch” would let you “use your phone number on up to two phones,” with the SIM automatically switching to “whichever phone you unlock.”

“Android Fast Switch” sounds nearly identical to Handoff on iPhone, a feature that lets iPhone owners share one SIM between two iPhones and quickly switch devices on the fly.

Other strings explicitly say that you can “Use a SIM on two phones” and specify that both devices would need to be logged into the same Google account. One phone would be the “Primary” with the other as a “companion.”

There are still plenty of unknown details here, including what data might sync between devices as well as how carriers will respond. On iPhone, carriers either charge extra for this feature ($5 at T-Mobile, $10 at Verizon), or don’t support it at all (AT&T). We also don’t know what devices may be supported – this could easily end up as a Pixel-only affair.

This would make my decision to buy device like Clicks Communicator much easier

As per usual with these early teardowns, though, the actual launch of a feature like this could be weeks, months, or even years away. Still, the idea itself is rather exciting.

iPhone’s Handoff feature always sounded useful, but I personally always questioned why it would be useful on iPhone. With so little variety in hardware, the utility of switching between two iPhones seems somewhat dubious, outside of perhaps using a Mini or Air as a situational device alongside a Pro device.

On Android, the combinations are endless. It all just depends on what devices Google supports. What devices would you switch between?

Nvidia is stuck between a rock and a hard place, as "irreplaceable talent" wants to leave if Jensen Huang isn't reinstated as CEO amidst the spy affair controversy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following the resignation of former CEO Jensen Huang, there are warnings that important Nvidia employees are planning to leave if the company doesn’t reinstate him. According to the Wall Street Journal, an employee sent an internal letter to the top executives and board of directors stating that “failure to reinstate Jensen Huang as chief executive could lead to the loss of ‘irreplaceable talent.’” Huang resigned last week amidst a firestorm of reports about an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, a further affair with VP of health care Kimberly Powell, and allegations the former relationship may have facilitated chip smuggling into China.

According to WSJ’s sources, director of developer technology Nader Khalil wrote the letter, urging that Huang be reinstated as CEO, lest the company lose loyal and important employees. The letter’s recipients, according to WSJ, comprised roughly 60 direct reports and the 10 members of the board, and Khalil warned that “Irreplaceable talent at Nvidia is now contemplating departing.”

The internal letter, along with the standwithjensen.com open letter to reinstate him, shows how important Huang is to the employees of Nvidia. Without the key long-term employees, which the company heavily depends on, progress could halt, or possibly regress, as Nvidia would lose the employees who have worked on and built the architectures and systems that make Nvidia what it is, along with their leader, Huang — who has recently stated that he would be honored to come back as CEO.

The open letter, signed by multiple Nvidia current and former employees, and tech CEOs and investors, states that employees are concerned that Huang was ousted unfairly and without due process, expressing that the employees do not have “access to the board’s private deliberations,” and that they have not been shown evidence that Alice Huang was indeed a spy, which even US intelligence doesn’t have solid evidence of. The open letter demands that Huang be reinstated as CEO while ensuring an independent investigation and a fair hearing, including providing a “specific, evidence-based explanation” of why Nvidia shouldn’t keep Huang as CEO — with Huang scheduled to appear before a House of Foreign Affairs panel concerning his affairs with the alleged spy.

Despite the letter, the support of other Silicon Valley CEOs, and even President Trump, Nvidia is still not considering bringing Huang back as CEO. Nvidia is the hottest AI hardware company at the moment, and its employees are highly desirable not only within the US, but possibly China as well. China surely wouldn’t mind getting its hands on a few key employees, who, if they choose to leave, are likely not going to have a hard time finding another job.

#10309. ‘It’s a risk’: How Rockstar built the most detailed (and romantic) GTA yet

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Producer and head of development Rob Nelson explains the studio’s approach on Grand Theft Auto VI.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI isn’t just the biggest GTA game to date; it’s also the most romantic. Though the series is well known for its sordid assortment of criminal leads, GTA VI switches things up by starring a pair of thieves who also happen to fall in love after meeting while stealing a car. It’s still an open-world action game full of chaos and mayhem. But many players will also be attempting to nurture the budding relationship in between heists. The sprawling game is designed to support both audiences.

“It’s a risk,” explains Rob Nelson, producer on GTA VI and co-studio head at developer Rockstar North. “It’s a real change for Grand Theft Auto. You can still play it the old way, you can reject all of this stuff. But for people who do engage, it’s meaningful — you’re earning it, you’re working at it, just like in a normal relationship.”

Despite the couple — named Jason and Lucia — being so central to the GTA VI experience, the development of the game didn’t start with characters or story. It began with a place. Nelson says that “pretty quickly” the team settled on placing the game in Leonida, the GTA universe’s version of Florida and the setting of one of the series’ most iconic games, Vice City on the PS2. In 2015, a small group of developers from Rockstar headed to Florida to get a sense of the place and set the groundwork for what would go on to become GTA VI.

“We just went around to feel it out,” explains Nelson. “Go to the Everglades, go to the Keys, go to the city, as many places as we can, and start thinking about what aspects of the place we want to incorporate. And then the map team starts building our version of that and talking very loosely and very broadly about what types of people we want to include and what types of places.”

The team realized they wanted to tell a story that would pull players through the various locations they were creating. And the best way to do that was to have characters who were relatively small-time criminals. They would start near the bottom of the criminal underworld and work their way up, traversing Leonida’s various locations, culminating in the splashy Vice City.

At the same time, the developers also wanted to do something different with the character-switching mechanic from GTA V, which let players swap between three unique criminals, each with very different stories and motivations. For GTA VI, a couple working together to steal enough money to escape the outlaw life fit both of these ideas perfectly. But it also presented some new challenges.

“Could we make a relationship work?” Nelson remembers thinking. “It’d be very different from three characters living separate lives that come together for narrative purposes and then split. These two are going to need to be together all the time and that’s going to be a challenge. It felt really wrong to have these people that are supposedly together only be together when we’re giving you scripted content.”

While GTA V is the obvious reference point, the new GTA also builds off of Rockstar’s 2018 Western Red Dead Redemption 2. That game was notable in particular for giving players more ways to interact with the world: You could talk to any NPC, sit around a campfire while drinking coffee or playing cards, or grow out your beard for a new look.

GTA VI expands this in ways that are designed to reinforce the relationship aspect of the experience. For instance, not only can you drink coffee, but you can also make a cup and hand it to your partner. The two can spend time together sitting on the couch watching TV, or go out to a club and dance. There’s even a button dedicated exclusively to making Jason and Lucia hold hands when they’re together.

“If it’s additive, and it makes you laugh, we include it.”

Nelson notes that when the team implemented the hand-holding feature, it was a revelatory moment for showing how the story and gameplay could connect. “When we got that mechanic in — and it’s not just a toggle, it’s an active thing that you have to commit to and hold — it was a really profound thing,” he says.

But it’s also a careful balancing act. While RDR2 was a widely praised game, it also received criticism for its slow pace, which was due in large part to all of the small mechanical details. These added animations and button prompts that gave texture to the world but could also lead to a plodding pace. That would be a much bigger problem in a game like GTA, which is built around action. Nelson says that he can be “freakishly obsessive” about these kinds of details, but he and the team had to learn to let go when realism got in the way of the game itself.

“Maybe you don’t have to carry around antifreeze in your trunk so that if your engine starts smoking you can top it up,” he says. “Certain things fall by the wayside in developing the game, where it’s like, we don’t need this. But other things, like eating food, it was important for us. You want to feel like you are this person existing in this world to a degree. That felt like it was additive. I love walking down the street with a beer and a cigarette and switching between the two, feeling like a dude. If it’s additive, and it makes you laugh, we include it.”

This also led the team down some unexpected paths. For instance, GTA VI is the first game in the series with functioning bedsheets. In past titles, and in basically every other video game, characters simply lay on top of a bed to go to sleep. That’s because it’s a lot of effort to both animate a character going under the covers as well as rig the sheets so that characters can interact with them in a way that looks and feels natural. The payoff isn’t worth it. But in the context of GTA VI, where the characters hide out together in everything from dingy motel rooms to fancy hotels, it made sense to actually build it.

“Because you’re sharing a living space, and you come in and see your partner sleeping at 3AM, it’s weird to see them laying on the top of the bed all the time,” Nelson says. “Nobody does that. It’s a lot of work to rig the covers on all of the different beds and have them both be able to get in and out of it and be able to be under the covers together.”

Bedsheets, holding hands, cups of coffee — they’re seemingly small details in a world as large as Grand Theft Auto VI, one that encourages players to run wild. But they’re also indicative of the approach Rockstar took with this latest GTA and how the studio attempted to make every element work in concert, even if it required a lot of extra work, like the bed covers. “That’s an example of something that was a lot of work, but it was worth it.”

#10308. Find Orphan Pages by Comparing Your Sitemap with an Internal Crawl

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A page can return 200 OK, exist in an XML sitemap, and still be practically invisible to anyone browsing the site.

You do not need a full commercial crawler to find the obvious cases. A small Node.js script can compare two things:

Anything present in the first set but missing from the second deserves investigation.

You now have URLs that the application publishes through its sitemap but that your HTML crawl could not reach.

The page might be intentionally isolated. It might exist for a campaign. Navigation might depend on JavaScript. Your crawler may have missed authentication or pagination.

Should a normal visitor reasonably be able to discover this page from another page on the site?

Google recommends using standard crawlable elements with resolvable href values for links that should be discoverable. Google's guidance on crawlable links

That is also good web architecture independent of SEO.

Internal links should not exist merely to move ranking signals around.

They should help users move between related tasks.

A technical guide might lead to a product page. A product page might link back to the configuration guide. A category page should make important child resources discoverable without requiring a user to know the exact URL.

This is why on-page SEO and internal linking are closely connected to site architecture. Good internal links clarify relationships for both visitors and crawlers.

At a broader level, crawlability is one part of technical SEO and search visibility. A page that nobody can reliably discover is difficult to evaluate regardless of how strong its content may be.

If an important URL appears disconnected because navigation is created only after client-side JavaScript runs, the script has surfaced a real architectural fact.

You can then decide whether that is acceptable.

If your application genuinely requires rendered-DOM crawling, replace the fetch-and-Cheerio layer with Playwright.

Do not make the simple crawler pretend to be something it is not.

The best output from this tool is not an "SEO score."

It is a concrete question for the development team:

This page exists. The sitemap knows about it. Why can't a normal HTML crawl reach it?

#10307. Samsung’s Galaxy S27 redesign is ‘100%’ confirmed, leaker says – do you like it? [Poll]

Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series is getting a redesign, with a reliable leaker saying it’s definitely happening. But, do you like it?

To say that Galaxy smartphone design has been stale is a bit of an understatement. The same core design has been in use for over half a decade at this point, with only the smallest tweaks in the past few generations. And it’s been a problem, too. While Samsung insists that the vertical triple camera array is the company’s “core identity,” it also means that the industry’s move to magnetic wireless charging and accessories are essentially incompatible with Galaxy devices.

We’ve seen repeated leaks of the Galaxy S27 series so far with a new full-width camera island on the Ultra and Pro models, and leaker Ice Universe says that this design is now “100%” confirmed, with the final design having two larger camera lenses on the left side, a smaller lens on the bottom right, and a flash above that. We saw this same design in a leak earlier this month, which Ice said at the time was “wrong,” presumably because the flash was replaced by a fourth camera in the dummy unit.

But the question at this point is, do you even like this new look?

Personally, I’m on the fence. I’m thrilled Samsung is finally doing away with the table-rocking, Qi2-ruining vertical array, but I’m not exactly happy we’re getting an iPhone clone as a result. I’d say beggars can’t be choosers, but this is Samsung we’re talking about – I think it’s fair to ask for a bit more.

The best budget-friendly gifts for gamers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The gamers in your life might be hoping for a $7,000 graphics card (I know I am), but that doesn't mean they won't still appreciate some solid, significantly cheaper gaming peripherals. While $80 doesn't go quite as far as it used to, there are still plenty of awesome accessories that any gamer would be thrilled to receive — including headsets, mice, keyboards, microphones, mouse pads, and even an Elgato Stream Deck.

Budget-friendly doesn't mean compromising on quality or convenience, either. While you won't be able to find all the bells and whistles at the sub-$80 level, there are both wired and wireless headsets, mice, and keyboards at this price point, including some last-generation flagship products. Razer's DeathAdder V3 Pro is currently on sale for $64.99, and remains one of the best-feeling mice we've ever tested — and its shape was so good that Razer kept it, exactly, in the newer DeathAdder V4 Pro. HyperX's Cloud III gaming headset is basically identical to our favorite Cloud III S headset — the only real difference is that the Cloud III is wired, while the Cloud III S is wireless.

The HyperX Cloud III is a wired gaming headset with a detachable microphone. It features 53mm drivers and virtual surround sound support.

This wireless lightweight over-ear headset weighs just 212g and is very comfortable. It features very good embedded beamforming mics and gets up to 24 hours of battery life on a single charge.

The G203 is now on sale for under $19 and is a great option for a budget mouse. It has six programmable buttons, and an 8K DPI optical sensor. There's also a RGB strip that wraps around the backside and shows off the Logitech "G" for a bit of bling.

Sporting a 12K sensor, a 99g weight, and a 250-hour battery rating on a single AA battery, Logitech's G503 wireless isn't an ultra-light 8K polling elite gaming mouse. But it solid experience while gaming and working. Unless you're playing competitive titles on an elite level, it just might be all the gaming mouse you really need.

Save an incredible 47% and get the Logitech G309 Lightspeed on sale for only $42.50. The G309 connects via Bluetooth or Wireless, uses a Hero 25K DPI sensor, six programmable buttons, and Lightforce mechanical switches.

The DeathAdder V3 Pro is a lightweight wireless gaming mouse with a fantastic shape, Razer's Focus Pro 30K optical sensor, and up to 90 hours of battery life. It weighs just 63g and is perfect for a range of gamers.

This Razer wireless gaming mouse features the company's Focus Pro 30K DPI optical sensor. It has a battery life of 280 hours using a single AA battery.

A wired membrane keyboard that punches above its weight: features SteelSeries' Whisper-Quiet membrane switches, 8 zones of RGB lighting, and a compact TKL form factor. It's also water- and dust-resistant.

Hexgears Immersion A3 combines the small footprint of a TKL keyboard with low-profile keycaps and switches, and multi-connectivity with 2.4GHz wireless, Bluetooth, plus a wired connection. There's even a small TFT screen to monitor battery life or host a personalized GIF.

The Logitech G413 TKL SE Mechanical Gaming Keyboard is a wired device with backlit keys. As the name suggests, it has a tenkeyless form factor so there is no numeric pad. The keys are mechanical with tactile switches.

This budget-friendly gaming mic features a unique ball shape and sounds fantastic out of the box. It comes with a sturdy desktop stand and features two mounting points so you can mount it at an angle, as well as a cardioid polar pattern and fun RGB lighting.

A USB condenser mic with a cardioid polar pattern, excellent sound out of the box, and a built-in desktop stand with a mounting point on the bottom. It also has a large tap-to-mute button on the top, and a red indicator light so you know when the mic is recording.

These 2.0 desktop speakers have a unique, round pebble shape with angled drivers so the sound hits your ears just right. They feature both wired and Bluetooth connections, have impressive bass for their size, and they've even got customizable RGB lighting around the base.

The Stream Deck Mini features six buttons but is otherwise compatible with the full suite of Stream Deck tools from Elgato.

SteelSeries' classic QcK gaming mouse pad is fairly basic — featuring a micro-woven cloth surface and a nonslip rubber backing, and measuring 12.6 x 10.8 inches (320 x 270mm) and just 2mm thick — but it's a solid option if you're looking for a no-frills mouse pad to up your mouse accuracy.

This XL extended mouse pad has a cloth surface that feels great — almost like a cooling pad. It features a non-slip rubber backing and a gunmetal gray pattern that may not be for everyone.

SteelSeries' performance-specific mouse pads are designed for specific scenarios — the balance mouse pad has a soft cloth surface designed to give you a mix of speed and precision.

#10303. XREAL Aura Initial Review: Taking Android XR out of the headset, but who is it for?

We all kind of want to be Tony Stark, don’t we? Almost everyone can relate to that futuristic sense of wonder that comes from the movies where we see digital elements being interacted with through a simple wave of the hand. To date, that’s only really been replicable through big ol’ headsets like Apple’s Vision Pro or the various Meta Quest sets. But with XREAL Aura, Android XR brings that feeling to something far easier to actually use, but my one remaining question is who this product is for.

XREAL isn’t new to the mixed reality space. The company has been building some of the best example of optical see-through XR glasses. These aren’t quite like a VR headset because they actually let you see the world around you, not through a camera, but by simply making the lenses themselves see-through. I’ve tried these in the past and, while they’re great for mirroring a tablet or laptop for bigger screen (especially on airplanes), I did feel they were missing something.

The other half of the formula is a wired puck that provides battery power and the brains of Android XR. There’s no screen on the puck, just a status light, and it has USB-C out for the glasses as well as USB-C in for power and/or external devices. It also has a fingerprint sensor (are you hearing this Android tablet makers??) and a touchpad, the latter used as a secondary, far subtler input method within Android XR to supplement hand tracking.

Together, these two make a wonderful package for building a true mixed reality experience. The displays are plenty bright for even the brightest indoor conditions (frankly, it’d be insane to use these outside in my opinion, though a wide-open plane window may present a challenge), and the electrochromic dimming does a great job of blocking out most, but not quite all of the world around you. If you’re looking to be fully cut off from reality as you would with a VR headset, these aren’t for you. I did find some display elements, like smaller text, a little tough to focus my eyes on at times, but I have a feeling that’s more so me being overdue for an eye exam than it is the glasses themselves. There’s virtually no flicker in my usage, with only bright white backgrounds occasionally being a little overbearing, and everything is smooth with the 120Hz refresh rate.

Where this hardware really shines compared to a typical VR headset is in its design, and it’s what makes XREAL a perfect partner for Android XR. The glasses themselves weigh in at 95 grams, a mere fifth of the Meta Quest 3 and Samsung’s Galaxy XR, and up to 700 grams less than Apple’s Vision Pro. It’s comfortable for much longer periods of time and not nearly as annoying to actually put on either, as it just feels like putting on a pair of bulky sunglasses. They look a little goofy, obviously, but far less so than a full-on VR headset like any of those mentioned above. This is also the only one of those I consider “mixed reality” thanks to the see-through lenses. Apple and Samsung constantly talk about Vision Pro and Galaxy XR as if they’re mixed reality, but a camera feed is explicitly not true reality. But XREAL’s glasses still aren’t quite just augmented reality, because they’re not just letting you see that digital world through a tiny window, they’re quite literally overlaying everything on top of the real world around you. The illusion is somewhat broken by the limited 70-degree field-of-view and the fact that darker backgrounds often look a little funky when there are light sources in the real world in front of you, but I can say with confidence that this is my favorite version of this experience – the speakers are pretty good too, topping it all off.

Google’s platform launched on Samsung’s Galaxy XR headset earlier this year, and it’s all pretty familiar on XREAL Aura. The Android-based experience opens up to a floating launcher of your favorite apps that mostly open up into floating windows in front of you. You can use hand gestures to move everything around or interact with UI elements, and every app I tried worked perfectly well. The gestures can be hit or miss at times, especially the one for going back to the home screen (having this as a long-press that you have to physically look at your hand for gets annoying when doing anything remotely fast-paced, but thankfully the top button is a dedicated home shortcut), but it works well and everything is designed fairly intuitively. A floating cursor shows where you are aiming with a pinch acting as a tap on a touchscreen, and you can move things around in your space as if they’re real objects. There’s a sense of depth throughout the entire experience.

Some apps open to fully immersive experiences, but these are still in the minority. XREAL touts that there are around 150 experiences launching alongside Aura, but it’s definitely still early days here. Apps like Google TV should obviously be ready for a full format like that but, most things, like the Google Docs app which I’m typing this review in right now, just act as if they’re on independent displays floating around you.

Like Googlebook, Android XR is also still struggling to figure out the best app experience for each user. Most things are mobile apps, including Google Chrome which is just the standard Android version from your phone. It’s fine in most cases, but I found this restrictive seeing as I can get a ton done in a browser, but fighting mobile optimization is a frustrating way to interact with a window that can be as big as a TV if you want it to. Meanwhile, the Spotify app is weirdly the web version which is horribly optimized for interacting with hand gestures. I’d have rather had the mobile version here. Another quirk I found was notifications, which pop up at the top of the display and quite literally on top of whatever you’re doing. Something they were invasive, and having to physically reach out to dismiss them is a little annoying. I’m sure there are workflows to help make this less of a distraction, but I’d almost rather have notifications turned off entirely.

There’s also Gemini, which is core to the experience here. A quick “Hey Google” triggers a Gemini Live session for answering questions, and you can share what you’re seeing to give additional context. It’s way faster than switching between apps to get a quick search done, but there are friction points here, especially if you’re just asking a single question. Gemini answers quickly and leaves the conversation going, great for follow-up queries, bad if you’re done because the UI controls go away, which leaves you to tell Gemini to end the session which, every time, it asks for secondary confirmation you want to end. Google, if I say “end the session,” I think that should be more than sufficient proof I want to end the session. Luckily, a long-press of the top home button fixes that headache, but I still hate that I either need to use a physical control, or basically have to fight an AI to end a conversation that I started hands-free.

Performance is excellent. Everything is smooth and quick, probably thanks in part to XREAL’s use of 12GB of RAM alongside Qualcomm’s Snapdragon Reality Elite chip.

But how do you actually use a product like this?

That’s the question I found myself asking when I first spun up XREAL Aura and started to learn its ins and outs. See, other XREAL glasses have an extremely straightforward use case. Plug in the external device of your choice, get it on a bigger screen. Simple, easy, obvious. I found this helpful in travel primarily, with a bigger display for my MacBook feeling nice for tasks like video editing, while the main value came in just having a very comfortable way to watch a movie on a flight from my connected tablet.

Both of those use cases are valid on XREAL Aura. You can plug a USB-C device into the puck to get that wired mirroring through an Android XR window, and it works super well. Having Android XR alongside that mirroring also opens the door to added functionality, like having extra windows off to the side for perhaps a media app or reference document if you run out of room on the wired device. As just one example, I kept Spotify and Slack open in side windows while using Aura with my MacBook Air for an afternoon of writing articles.

I think Android XR makes Aura an absolute powerhouse for media consumption, as you can just download content directly to the device (256GB of storage in the base model) for a flight, or even just use streaming apps if you have Wi-Fi. I couldn’t find any 3D content during my brief review period (nothing in my Google TV library was compatible, apparently), but that’s also on the table. Genuinely, I cannot wait to use these on my next long flight.

But outside of those, I think a lot of the other scenarios start to fall apart.

By far the most popular use case for virtual/mixed reality has been gaming. XREAL Aura has a ton of potential here and I’m really excited to try out Project Hail Mary: Journey to the Stars on this, but the fact that these are simply glasses restricts a lot of what you can do. Any big movements could easily result in the glasses slipping or even falling off, whereas a VR headset is much more secure. Stationary immersive gaming has potential, and XREAL’s Gamesir controller collab shows promise, but I’m just not sold on these as a replacement to something like Meta Quest seeing as the latter already has a deep catalog of games and a much more affordable price.

The other use case is productivity, one I’m also not entirely sold on. As mentioned, this entire post was written in Aura, and I’m not sure I benefitted much from that experience versus just using a normal laptop or desktop computer. My workflow was having Google Docs up in the center, typing on a physical keyboard (only a psycho could use the virtual keyboard for anything beyond the shortest messages or passwords) with reference documents in Chrome off to my right and Slack on my left, with the occasional “Hey Google” command helping to find information on the fly (such as the weight comparison earlier). It all works well, but I think Android XR is inherently limited by its reliance mostly on mobile apps, and also just by the use of gestures. You can use a mouse or trackpad, of course, but at that point you might as well just use a laptop.

XREAL Aura is for a very specific customer from my point of view. Wired mirroring is still very much the strong suit here, with Android XR’s apps and offline video giving these utility outside of just relying on another device. That was my problem with previous XREAL glasses, because the experience just varies wildly depending on what’s connected. Aura’s puck and Android XR experience solves every complaint I had about previous models, while expanding very much on what it can do.

Further limiting matters is the price. $1,279, in my book, is actually a mostly fair price for this, especially given the RAMageddon of it all. XREAL 1+ Pro come in around $600 and is utterly useless without a secondary device. XREAL Aura more or less doubles the price, but includes the best possible version of that secondary device while also giving you the added camera sensors that are only available as add-ons for other hardware. Don’t get me wrong, it’s still pricey, but I don’t think it’s unfair either, especially not when one of the main competitors for this sort of experience is still $3,600 (which still sounds insane two years later, even for Apple).

We’ll have far more to say about XREAL Aura in a long-term review from our Nick Sutrich, but the verdict on day one is that Android XR has really solved the biggest problem for XREAL, while XREAL has solved a lot of big problems for Android XR, all while being an overall win for the user at the end of the day. Not so often we get a storybook ending like that in tech anymore, and I’m all here for it.

Don’t forget, XREAL Aura is $200 off – so $1,099 – through the end of December. Much more worth it at that price.

#10301. Oppo may finally be fixing broken 3rd-party launchers with ColorOS 17

Most Chinese-brand Android phones don’t like it when you use a 3rd-party launcher, but the ColorOS 17 update may be changing that.

If I hop on a phone from Oppo, Vivo, Honor, or most other Chinese brands and try to use a 3rd-party launcher, I’m definitely not going to like how the phone operates. Often, there’s a weird delay when navigating back home or opening the Overview multitasking screen. Sometimes, Overview doesn’t work at all, essentially forcing you back to the default launcher that shipped with the phone.

But one Reddit user says Oppo is doing things differently in the ColorOS 17 update. According to their experience, the delay and other issues with 3rd-party launchers are completely solved. OnePlus’s OxygenOS doesn’t have the same issues, so it’s good to see that OnePlus users who are still set to receive ColorOS 17 in the near future won’t feel like their phones are losing a valuable feature.

Chinese Android brands like Oppo, Vivo, Xiaomi, Realme, and several others often outshine Western brands in many ways, but they’ve always had one annoying weakness: software. Whether it’s the number of updates a phone receives, overall quality, or another specific issue, Chinese brands often build very different visions of Android than Google, Samsung, Motorola, or Nothing.

The value and hardware prowess these phones usually deliver help outshine software weaknesses, and these companies have been steadily improving software over the past year or two to meet the demands of a more Westernized audience. Companies like Honor now offer 7 years of software updates, and ColorOS is widely regarded as the best Android skin next to Samsung’s One UI in many circles. That’s great news for everyone, especially in an age where fewer smartphone companies compete for your hard-earned dollars.

The City of Memphis has announced that it will make its first major investment as part of its new Community Benefits Ordinance. The fund will use revenue generated by taxing SpaceX AI's Colossus 1 facility to fund various initiatives within the South Memphis neighborhoods around that data center. The fund will help with the costs associated with housing, neighborhood improvements, and services, with the first $2 million allocated for the Memphis HOPE Initiative — a new home repair assistance program that helps residents make vital repairs as well as improvements that will make their homes more accessible.

The HOPE Initiative will provide low- and moderate-income homeowners and even renters with up to $25,000 for qualifying repairs and improvements. Eligible improvements include structural repairs as well as plumbing, heating, and air conditioning repairs. Roof repairs and replacement, accessibility improvements, and some exterior improvements like painting, fencing, and landscaping are also eligible. Funds will be provided as a deferred, interest-free, forgivable loan with zero monthly payments. Any required lien will be recorded after repairs are completed and forgiven after three years.

A year later, in May 2026, it was announced that Anthropic had signed a lease that would see it take over the entire facility in an effort to ease usage bottlenecks across Claude's paid-for ecosystem. The company said that the additional compute afforded by Colossus 1 would enable higher Claude Code limits, the removal of throttling for Pro and Max customers, and increased API request limits for Claude Opus models. But none of that will matter to those benefiting from the Community Benefits Ordinance, of course.

#10298. What is Model Routing in AI?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.
