#10261. Top 6 AI Detector Tools in 2026: Features, Accuracy Claims and When to Trust Them

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI writing tools are now part of everyday work for students, marketers, developers and editors, and a parallel category of tools has appeared to answer one question: was this text written by a person or a model? Teachers, publishers, hiring teams and content managers use these detectors to screen submissions, but the technology is probabilistic, its accuracy claims vary, and the cost of a wrong call can be high.

This guide compares six AI detector tools, CudekAI, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Originality.ai, Turnitin and Sapling, on what their own pages state. It then explains how detectors work, summarizes the research on their limits, and ends with a short script you can use to test any detector on your own samples. The tools are listed in no ranking order. Feature details, limits and prices change often, so confirm current information on each vendor’s site before you commit.

• A detector gives a probability, not proof. Every vendor in this list warns that results can be wrong, and several advise against using a score alone.

• Limits differ widely. Character limits, supported languages, models detected and free allowances vary between tools and even between pages of the same tool.

• Bias and short text are known weak spots. Research has found detectors misclassify some non-native English writing, and most vendors say accuracy improves with longer text.

• Vendor accuracy claims are not comparable. Figures come from different datasets, thresholds and models, so test on your own material.

• Use detection as one signal. Combine it with drafts, version history, interviews or other evidence before acting on a result.

CudekAI’s AI detector returns an AI percentage and a human percentage for pasted text, with paragraph, sentence and word-level analysis for mixed documents. According to its page, it also accepts PDF and Word files with per-page scores and downloadable reports, and offers separate code and image detectors. The page lists models it says it detects, including ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Llama, DeepSeek and Grok, and says it supports more than 100 languages.

Strengths: sentence-level highlighting, file uploads with reports, and extra detectors for code and images in the same product.

Limitations and gaps: the page shows different character limits in different places, including 1,000, 5,000 and 15,000, so check the limit that applies to your plan. It says to start free but does not state a free allowance, and it lists detection as costing one credit. A headline accuracy figure of 99 percent appears in its page metadata, while the body refers to independent benchmarking without naming sources, and its FAQ says accuracy may vary for mixed or paraphrased text and that false positives can occur. API access is listed under an enterprise plan.

Fits: writers and teams who want a quick check with sentence-level detail and file reports, and who will verify results rather than rely on a single percentage.

GPTZero gives an overall score and highlights flagged sentences, and adds a Writing Replay feature that records the writing process, including typing patterns and paste events. It also lists a plagiarism checker and integrations with Google Docs, Google Classroom, Canvas, Zapier and a Chrome extension, plus an API.

Strengths: process-based evidence through Writing Replay, strong education integrations and published benchmark figures. On the RAID benchmark, the page reports 95.7 percent of AI text detected and 1 percent of human text wrongly flagged.

Limitations and gaps: it claims full support for five languages (English, German, Portuguese, French and Spanish) and says other languages lack stated accuracy levels. The company’s own page says no detector is 100 percent accurate, that results should not be the final verdict, and that accuracy is higher on longer English text. The free scan box has a 10,000-character limit, and plan prices are not listed on the page.

Fits: educators and editors who want writing-process evidence as well as a score.

Copyleaks describes an AI detector covering more than 30 languages and models including ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Llama. Its AI Logic feature explains why text was flagged, and it says it can detect AI-generated code. It offers a browser extension, a Google Docs add-on, learning-management-system integrations and an enterprise API.

Strengths: explanation of flagged text, code detection, broad integrations and published per-language accuracy figures, which range from 93.08 percent for Portuguese to 99.20 percent for English on AI text, according to the page.

Limitations and gaps: the figures are the vendor’s own. It claims a 0.03 percent false-positive rate and cites a 2023 study, so treat the number as a claim to verify on your data. Free scans cover up to 25,000 characters, but paid plan prices sit on a separate pricing page.

Fits: organizations that need multilingual coverage, code detection or integrations.

Originality.ai combines AI detection with a plagiarism checker and a fact and hallucination checker. It lists sentence-level highlights, a mixed AI and human category in its newer detection models, a Chrome extension with a writing replay feature and an AI detection API.

Strengths: several checks in one product, an API with documentation and a clear statement of limits. The page acknowledges false positives, says very short text under a 100-word minimum and AI rewriting tools can affect results, and advises against using the score alone.

Limitations and gaps: it is credit-based, free scans are capped at 100 credits per account and credits expire two years after purchase. Its accuracy claims cite third-party studies and internal benchmarks that you should review yourself.

Fits: publishers, agencies and SEO teams that want detection, plagiarism and fact checks in one workflow.

Turnitin is built for educators and institutions. Its AI writing detection estimates how much of a submission is human-written versus likely AI-generated, including likely AI-paraphrased content, and works inside learning-management systems alongside similarity checking.

Strengths: deep institutional integration and unusually explicit framing of results as probabilities. For documents above its 300-word minimum, the page reports false-positive rates of 0.014 for English language learners and 0.013 for native English writers and concludes there is no statistically significant bias. It also links guidance on handling suspected false positives.

Limitations and gaps: it is aimed at institutions rather than individual freelancers, the page does not give an overall false-positive figure, and it describes detection as just one piece of a broader challenge. Mention of language support is limited, with a linked post about Arabic.

Fits: schools and universities that already use Turnitin and want detection within the same workflow.

Sapling offers an AI content detector with a free check of the first 2,000 characters, and Pro and Enterprise accounts can check up to 100,000 characters. It provides a free API key and documentation, and it publishes unusually candid guidance on limits.

Strengths: transparent caveats and a developer-friendly API. The page says false positives and negatives may occur, that accuracy varies with dataset, text length and threshold, and advises measuring results on your own examples. It also warns that some tools claiming to be undetectable falsely say they passed its detector.

Limitations and gaps: accuracy improves after about 50 words, non-English text is scored by the English model for unsupported languages and is described as less reliable, and prices are not listed on the page. A reported 60 percent drop in false positives comes from internal evaluations that the page does not publish.

Fits: developers and teams that want to integrate detection and test it on their own data.

The table summarizes what each page states. It is not a benchmark, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

• False positives. Human writing can be flagged, especially when it is short, formulaic or highly predictable.

• Bias risk. A research paper titled GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers found that the detectors it tested consistently misclassified non-native English writing as AI-generated, and warned against using such detectors in educational settings. Turnitin’s page reports the opposite for its own tool, which is a reason to test on your population.

• Evasion. Paraphrasing or editing AI text can lower scores. The same research found that simple prompting strategies could both reduce bias and get text past detectors.

• Mixed authorship. A document that is partly AI-assisted can get a low overall score while individual sentences score high, as Sapling’s page points out.

• Moving targets. New models change the text distribution, so a tool trained on older models can lose accuracy.

• Shifting benchmarks. Vendors report different datasets and thresholds, so numbers are not comparable.

OpenAI’s own experience is a useful reminder. Its AI text classifier correctly flagged 26 percent of AI-written text on a challenge set while mislabeling 9 percent of human text, and the page states that the classifier was withdrawn on July 20, 2023 due to its low rate of accuracy. Detection has improved since, but the underlying difficulty has not gone away.

Most detectors are classifiers trained on large collections of human-written and machine-generated text. They look for statistical patterns, such as how predictable the word choices are or how uniform the sentence structure is, and output a probability. Some systems add features such as sentence-level scoring, writing-process data, or checks for hidden provenance signals.

• Statistical signals. Text that a language model finds highly predictable tends to look machine-like, which is why formulaic human writing can be misflagged.

• Trained classifiers. Detectors learn from labeled examples and update as new models appear.

• Writing-process evidence. Tools such as Writing Replay record how a document was typed or pasted, which is stronger evidence than a style score.

• Provenance. Standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to record the origin and edits of content when the creating tool supports them, which is different from guessing after the fact.

Because vendor figures are not comparable, the most reliable approach is to measure. Build a small labeled set that reflects your real content, including human writing from different groups of authors, and calculate false-positive and false-negative rates by group. The script below wraps any detector behind a single function. The stub is a placeholder, so replace it with a call to the tool you are evaluating and follow that vendor’s API documentation.

is_ai: bool        # ground truth you control

group: str = "all" # e.g. "native", "non-native", "technical"

def evaluate(detect, samples, threshold=0.5):

"""detect(text) -> probability the text is AI-generated (0..1).

Wrap any vendor API or tool export in this function."""

g = stats.setdefault(s.group, {"fp": 0, "human": 0, "fn": 0, "ai": 0})

g["fn"] += not flagged          # AI text that slipped through

g["fp"] += flagged              # human text wrongly flagged

fpr = g["fp"] / g["human"] if g["human"] else float("nan")

fnr = g["fn"] / g["ai"] if g["ai"] else float("nan")

print(f"{group:12} false positive rate {fpr:.1%}  false negative rate {fnr:.1%}")

return 0.9 if "delve" in text.lower() else 0.2

Sample("Let us delve into the topic.", True, "native"),

Sample("I wrote this note by hand.", False, "native"),

Sample("We delve into methods used here.", False, "non-native"),

Sample("The results were reviewed twice.", True, "non-native"),

With only a handful of samples the numbers mean little, so use a larger, representative set and report results per group, because an acceptable overall rate can hide a high rate for one group.

• Never treat a score as proof. Use it to start a conversation, not end one.

• Gather corroborating evidence. Drafts, version history, writing-process data and discussion with the author carry more weight than a percentage.

• Tell people how detection is used. Publish a policy so authors know what to expect and how to appeal.

• Check for bias. Test on writers like those you evaluate, including non-native speakers.

A tool that estimates how likely a piece of text is to have been generated by an AI model, usually as a percentage, often with sentence-level highlighting.

They can be useful, but accuracy depends on the text, length, language and threshold, and every vendor in this guide states that errors can occur. Test on your own samples.

Yes. False positives are acknowledged by vendors and documented in research, particularly for short or formulaic text and some non-native English writing.

Free tiers are fine for a quick check, but they have limits on length and features, and the result should not be the sole basis for a decision.

Sometimes. Paraphrasing and editing can lower scores, and partly AI-generated documents can produce mixed results.

A plagiarism checker looks for matches with existing sources. An AI detector estimates whether text resembles machine-generated writing, which is a different and less certain task.

AI detectors are useful screening tools, but none is a verdict. The six reviewed here differ in language coverage, limits, integrations, explainability and how candidly their pages describe error, and their accuracy figures come from different tests. Compare them on the factors that matter for your content, measure false positives on your own samples, and combine any score with other evidence before acting on it.

#10259. OpenAI releases GPT-6.2 Astra to regain performance lead from Claude Fable 5.2

The frontier model races continues. A week after the launch of Anthropic’s Claude 5.2 and Google’s Gemini 4 Hydrogen, OpenAI is releasing GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol as a “new standard for professional work.”

Stepping back in time a few days, Anthropic last week launched Claude Fable and Mythos 5.2. The company’s latest models are “designed to tackle the most daunting technical challenges with “improvements in areas like coding, reasoning, and vision.” Anthropic says that Fable 5.2 beats Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra, and Gemini 4 Carbon virtually across the board in benchmarks including coding, cybersecurity, knowledge work, and more.

But, now, OpenAI is stepping up to the challenge.

OpenAI shows that GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are more or less playing ball with Anthropic’s latest models, but often delivering similar performance at a drastically lower cost.

Of the two new models, OpenAI says that Sol is “smarter, more efficient, and lets you complete more tasks on the same paid plan” as a “go-to model for everyday work.”

Astra, meanwhile, is tuned for the highest-quality work in professional use cases such as documents, slides, coding, and more:

GPT‑6.2 Astra sets a new quality bar for AI in complex professional work. Its slides, spreadsheets, and documents read less like AI, and more closely follow the templates you provide and the conventions of your field. It writes cleaner code and builds frontends that look and work better, particularly for websites, gaming, and 3D design.

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are now available to all Plus, Pro, Enterprise, and Edu users in ChatGPT Work and Codex, with Astra being significantly more affordable compared to GPT-6 Astra. Pricing for Astra per million tokens lands at $8 for input, $0.40 for cached input, and $40 for output. Sol matches its predecessor at $2 for input, $0.10 for cached input, and $10 for output, again per million tokens.

#10256. WhatsApp starts rolling out built-in cloud backup on Android, and it’s free

Meta’s WhatsApp is making a huge change to chat backups, with a new built-in cloud backup option on Android that doesn’t rely on Google, and is free.

Spotted by WABetaInfo, WhatsApp for Android is adding its own cloud backup option. Prior to this, the app relied on Google Drive backup for transferring chats between devices or keeping a backup in case of accidental deletion.

“WhatsApp cloud storage” is showing up in the WhatsApp for Android v2.26.46.4 beta update, rolling out now, and includes 2GB of free storage for cloud backups. Everything is still end-to-end encrypted. Beyond the first 2GB, WhatsApp Plus ($2.99/month) adds 100GB, and Meta One ($7.99/month+) offers up to 4TB of cloud storage. WhatsApp will keep your backup for at least 4 months after the subscription ends, even if you are over the 2GB gap.

You can still choose to use Google Drive backups instead of the built-in option, but those using devices without a Google account or who are running low on Google storage may appreciate the option to switch.

It’s unclear exactly when this will be available to all WhatsApp users.

#10254. Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

#10252. How Much Latency Does a Self-Hosted LLM Gateway Add? Langfuse vs LiteLLM vs AcruxCore

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A self-hosted LLM gateway sits between your app and the model provider, so every call pays for one extra hop. We measured that cost for three open-source gateways: the Langfuse AI Gateway, the LiteLLM proxy and AcruxCore. Each one ran on the same machine, in front of the same OpenAI model. LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to every non-streaming call. AcruxCore added 15 to 24 ms. Langfuse's number could not be told apart from zero.

The bigger lesson was about the measurement itself. Before we controlled for it, one OpenAI network address moved our results by more than any gateway did.

Each number is the median time a gateway added to a direct OpenAI call, with its 95% interval in brackets. A gateway can log each call, and logging takes time, so we ran the test three times with three logging levels.

The direct call took 781 to 831 ms at the median, so even LiteLLM's cost is under 9% of a short call. Read the tables this way:

The obvious test is to time 100 calls through the gateway, time 100 direct calls, and compare the two medians. That test gives the wrong answer, for two reasons.

The first reason is noise. OpenAI's own response time moved by hundreds of milliseconds between calls in our runs. The direct call's p95 was 1,047 to 1,233 ms against a median near 800 ms. A gateway that adds 20 ms is hidden inside that spread unless the test removes it.

The second reason is the network path to OpenAI. It does not stay the same between a direct client and a gateway, and that difference can be larger than the gateway itself.

api.openai.com resolves to more than one address. From our network, DNS returned two Cloudflare addresses, and one was much farther away than the other. We measured both with a script that pins one address at a time:

On a connection that is already open, both addresses answer in the same time. On a new connection, the far address adds about 250 ms, because the TCP and TLS handshakes take that much longer.

A gateway keeps its connection to OpenAI open between calls. It pays the setup once and then stays on whichever address it reached first. A client that opens a new connection for each call pays the setup again on every call. How much it pays depends on which address DNS returns that time.

Node's built-in fetch closes an idle connection after about 4 seconds. So a test client that calls OpenAI every few seconds reconnects on almost every call. In our first, uncontrolled run, the Langfuse gateway came out 43 ms faster than calling OpenAI directly. A gateway cannot be faster than the call it forwards. The direct client paid for new connections, and the gateway did not.

The fix has two parts. First, pin api.openai.com to one address on every path. In Docker, that is an extra_hosts entry on each gateway container:

Second, let the direct client keep its connection open, as a gateway does. With undici, the HTTP client behind Node's fetch, that is a custom Agent:

We then listed each container's open connections to confirm that every path used the same address. The edge-check script is in the repository linked at the end, and it takes about a minute to run from your own network.

This advice also applies outside benchmarks. If your app calls OpenAI directly from Node, keep the connection open between calls. Otherwise some of your calls pay the handshake, and on a far address that costs about 250 ms.

Each test sent 150 rounds after 5 warm-up rounds. One round calls OpenAI directly and through each gateway, once without streaming and once with streaming. The order rotates every round, so no path always goes first or last.

For each round, the script takes the gateway's time minus the direct call's time in that same round. Calls in one round are made within a few seconds of each other, so a slow moment at OpenAI hits all four paths together and cancels out of the difference. The script then reports the median of the 150 differences.

The interval comes from a bootstrap. The script draws 150 differences at random, with replacement, and takes their median. It repeats that 2,000 times, and the middle 95% of those medians is the interval. This is a shortened version of the script's function:

An interval that includes zero means the test could not separate the gateway's cost from zero. That is what the Langfuse rows show. It does not prove that the cost is zero.

Every call used OpenAI gpt-6-luna with reasoning_effort: "none", the same short prompt and a cap of 5 output tokens. A short answer keeps the model's own time small, so more of the measured time belongs to the path. Each streamed call measured the time to its first token. Each non-streamed call measured the time to the full response.

All three gateways did real work on every call, which made the comparison fair:

The three logging levels mapped to these settings:

The machine was an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with 62 GB of RAM, running Fedora Linux and Docker 29.9. All three stacks ran on it at once, and the test was run on 10 November 2026.

LiteLLM added 61 to 67 ms to the full response, but only 9 to 43 ms to the first streamed token. The first token arrives as soon as the model starts to answer, so time spent before that point shows up in both numbers. The larger cost without streaming suggests that most of LiteLLM's extra time is spent after the model has answered. We did not profile LiteLLM to confirm this.

Logging level made no clear difference for any gateway. Adding LiteLLM's Langfuse callback did not raise its cost. AcruxCore's median rose from 15 ms with logging off to 24 ms with full logging, but its intervals overlap across all three runs. AcruxCore's median is 16 to 24 ms above Langfuse's, and those intervals overlap too, so this test does not rank the two of them.

If 60 ms per call matters to you, as in a chat app that makes several model calls per user message, LiteLLM's cost is real and repeatable. Langfuse and AcruxCore stayed near or within the noise. If your calls take several seconds, any of the three costs less than OpenAI's own variation from one call to the next.

Before you trust a gateway number, your own or anyone else's, check two things. Was each gateway compared with a direct call in the same round? And did the direct client keep its connection open on the same OpenAI address as the gateway? Without both, the result measures the network path and not the gateway.

The scripts, Compose files and raw results are on GitHub: scripts/comparison/langfuse-gateway-vs-litellm-vs-acruxcore.

Talha Anwar is a senior AI and full-stack engineer and writes about building and running AI systems.

Price cuts and a lack of other good places to spend upgrade money are probably big factors.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Global OLED monitor shipments doubled year over year in the third quarter of 2026, according to the latest market research from TrendForce. Shipments also increased 13% sequentially, reflecting strong demand for OLED displays and improving panel availability. While the market's rapid growth is impressive regardless, there are good reasons why OLED monitors are becoming increasingly popular: they're getting cheaper, better, and considerably easier to justify.

ASUS maintained its position as the world's largest OLED monitor vendor, accounting for 21.7% of shipments. MSI climbed into second place for the first time, capturing 14.5% of the market, while Samsung slipped to third with 11.3%. Both Taiwanese vendors have been particularly aggressive in the gaming monitor market, where increasingly competitive pricing has helped make OLED technology accessible to more buyers, while ubiquitous advertising with tech-conscious gamers has made them highly visible. According to Omdia, mainstream 27-inch QHD 240Hz OLED gaming monitors have seen price cuts exceeding 40% in 2026.

Price isn't the only obstacle that OLED manufacturers have been addressing, though. Earlier generations of OLED monitors had some fairly significant compromises, particularly their relatively limited brightness and unconventional subpixel arrangements that could cause colored fringing around text. Newer tandem OLED panels offer improved brightness and color reproduction, while RGB-stripe OLED layouts bring much cleaner text rendering. These improvements are making OLEDs considerably more attractive as everyday desktop monitors, rather than merely excellent gaming displays with major caveats.

There's also an interesting macroeconomic angle to OLED's growth. The AI industry's insatiable appetite for memory and storage has made upgrading a gaming PC considerably more expensive. DRAM component prices have increased severalfold, while NAND flash and hard drive availability have also been squeezed by demand from AI data centers—though there are early signs of some relief, at least on the flash memory front. Graphics cards, especially (but not exclusively) at the high end, are increasingly difficult to find at anything resembling reasonable prices.

Monitors, meanwhile, aren't directly subject to the same supply pressures affecting DRAM, NAND, and discrete GPUs. That makes upgrading a display an appealing alternative for gamers who aren't particularly interested in spending a small fortune on an entirely new PC. An OLED monitor can substantially improve the gaming experience without requiring a new graphics card, memory kit, or storage drive. The contrast, response times, and color gamut of OLEDs far outstrip even the best LCDs, and that's to say nothing of their superior HDR reproduction thanks to the inherent self-emissive nature of OLED panels.

With OLED prices falling and the technology's longstanding limitations becoming less noticeable, a new monitor is becoming one of the more compelling upgrades available to PC gamers. Indeed, several of our picks for the best gaming monitors, including the best overall, best 4K, and best ultrawide, are based on OLED technology. Though concerns about burn-in and limited lifespan remain, there's no question that OLED is the best display technology we have right now.

#10249. Google confirms huge list of new Home features, including three Nest Thermostat upgrades

Google Home is constantly rolling out new updates and, today, the company is detailing some of its latest updates including a faster Gemini for Home experience and an upcoming Nest Thermostat upgrade.

In a post today, Google details several Google Home updates that are widely rolling out to users.

The first was announced last week – home keys in Google Wallet. This leverages compatible NFC and UWB (Aliro) locks on newer Pixel and select Samsung Galaxy smartphones, letting you unlock your door by simply tapping your phone.

Cameras are also getting an upgrade for Activity Zones:

…we’ve tightened Activity Zone boundary precision on our newest wired cameras to significantly reduce false alerts from passing cars, animals, and motion just outside your zone borders. We also redesigned the camera settings page to put Activity Zones front and center, and added a confirmation prompt on iOS when exiting an edited zone so you never accidentally lose your custom boundaries.

The Google Home website also gets faster scrubbing and new keyboard shortcuts – Left or Right arrow keys skip back or forward in 5-second increments, rapidly to jump 30 seconds or more.

Gemini for Home continues to get faster, with Google saying that the latest updates have resulted in an overall speed improvement of 20% on “everyday requests.” Gemini for Home has also expanded to six new countries including India, Taiwan, Singapore, Colombia, South Korea, and Brazil starting “next month.”

Starting in December, Nest thermostat owners in the U.S. and Canada can check the app to see estimated cumulative savings. And they can also balance hot and cold spots with compatible third-party room sensors with their 4th-gen Nest Learning Thermostat.

A third update is to the Dynamic Farsight faces on the latest Nest Learning Thermostat. “New” faces are coming with “designs to match your aesthetics,” while a new Lunar face will track moon phases.

Dynamic Farsight already lets you see the time, temperature, weather, and air quality from across the room, and starting this December, we’re rolling out new Dynamic Farsight faces with designs that match your aesthetics. #10247. The Downfall of Jensen Huang — a timeline of allegations and scandals that brought down the king of AI infrastructure

Affairs, geopolitics, and the global AI race. Plus a whole lot of money.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia rose from a niche gaming hardware company with an unofficial motto of "Our company is thirty days from going out of business” to becoming the world’s most valuable company by market capitalization in 2024, and co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang was the man behind its meteoric rise.

He planned and founded the company in 1993 alongside Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem while sitting inside a Denny’s diner in the Silicon Valley area. After more than 30 years, Nvidia had thousands of employees, a global footprint, and a name that people equated to AI chips.

All this came crumbling down for the billionaire founder in a matter of weeks, but the cracks started long before the first accusation. Let’s go back and see how it all began. This is an ongoing saga, so we have listed stories related to the scandal below, which we will update as events unfold.

Jensen resigned from Nvidia on November 12, after two weeks of news reports accusing him of multiple affairs, one with an alleged Chinese spy and another with his own subordinate. This is significant for Nvidia, which has had a single CEO since its inception. Huang was the face of the company, and many of its employees, shareholders, partners, and fans still support him despite the circumstances of his departure. He steered Nvidia into the giant that it is today by having the vision to develop CUDA nearly 20 years before ChatGPT made AI accessible to the average user and buying companies that helped it scale deployments to hundreds of thousands of AI GPUs.

By 2022, ChatGPT’s arrival and subsequent boom set the AI race in motion, with tech giants and AI startups buying up as many GPUs as they could get their hands on. The increased demand for Nvidia’s products caused it to break the trillion-dollar mark in less than a year after the release of OpenAI’s LLM. As the largest individual shareholders of the company, Huang and his wife, Lori, became some of the wealthiest people in the world.

The billionaire founder hasn't always gloated about his position. He once said in a podcast that if he could turn back time, he wouldn’t have founded Nvidia at all, and also cringed at the praises that the Caltech Board of Trustees chairperson heaped on him before he gave the 2024 keynote speech at the institute’s commencement exercises. He has frequently cited his history working as a “Dishwasher, Busboy, Waiter” at Denny’s, noting it under his experience on his LinkedIn page. More than that, people have seen Jensen go where other celebrity CEOs are rarely seen — enjoying street food in Vietnam, dining at a local fried chicken joint in Korea, and even dragging along 35 of Taiwan’s semiconductor CEOs to a restaurant in Taipei.

But even as he’s building his “Man of the People” persona, his position as the head of arguably the most important chipmaker in the AI industry meant that he was also gaining influence in business and governance. Industry leaders noticed the power that Nvidia held over those who want GPUs to train their AI models, with some of his fellow billionaires begging him over dinner to sell them GPUs. Just weeks into President Donald Trump’s second term, Jensen Huang was meeting with him at the White House, discussing AI policy and hardware export controls — something the Biden administration started in 2022 in an attempt to hold back China’s ambition'sin AI.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The rivalry between the U.S. and China has been simmering for years, with Chinese companies like Huawei getting blacklisted because of their work with the People’s Liberation Army. In August 2022, about three months before ChatGPT made AI a household term, Washington expanded its trade bans on Beijing and imposed export controls on Nvidia’s H100 and A100 GPUs. The company responded to this by building a new data center GPU that conformed to U.S. standards specifically for the Chinese market, called the A800, by November of that year. Despite delivering only 70% of the performance of the A100 it was based on, the GPU reportedly still saw great demand from Chinese customers.

However, the A800 only had a relatively short run as the Biden administration once again increased the performance threshold in October 2023, which resulted in the export prohibition of A800 and H800 GPUs to China. This move forced the company to iterate once again, creating new models that it could sell to Chinese companies, like the Nvidia H20. These chips arrived on the market in early 2024, but before the Biden administration ended in January 2025, it proposed even stricter rules called the AI Diffusion Policy. This sorted nations into different tiers based on trust and risk levels — countries in tier 1, which includes the U.S. and its closest allies, have minimal restrictions, while those in tier 3 are effectively barred from receiving advanced AI GPUs containing U.S. technology.

This is problematic for Nvidia, as China is the second-largest computer market in the world t and represented between 20% and 25% of Nvidia's data center sales. The company lobbied the U.S. government to ease AI GPU export restrictions, with Trump reportedly suspending the proposed Nvidia H20 ban after Jensen spent $1 million to have dinner with the president in early April 2025. The White House went ahead anyway and banned the sale of the AI GPU less than a week after that, resulting in a $5.5 billion write-off for the company.

Jensen Huang himself had harsh comments about Washington’s trade ban, saying that “export control was a failure,” and that it had caused the company’s AI GPU market share in China to go from 95% to zero. He even argued that “conceding an entire market the size of China probably does not make a lot of strategic sense, so I think that has already largely backfired.”

But even as Huang was fighting to have the trade ban on the Nvidia H20 chip lifted, he continued praising Trump’s tariff plan, calling it “utterly visionary.” The president eventually reversed the ban three months later, and by December 2025, he had approved Nvidia H200 exports to China, but only if Nvidia pays a 25% fee. The newer Blackwell and Vera Rubin chips remain out of reach for Chinese companies, though — at least on paper.

Allegations that Chinese companies have been breaking Washington’s trade ban and smuggling Nvidia AI GPUs into China have been circulating since June 2023, after Reuters revealed that vendors in Shenzhen could secure A100 chips when asked discreetly. They reportedly bought these chips from the excess stocks sold in the open market or through intermediaries in India, Taiwan, and Singapore. Another story published in January 2024 asserted that the Chinese military and related institutions, state-run data centers, and even universities continue buying banned A100 and H100 chips. Six months after that, accounts of underground networks dedicated to smuggling AI GPUs — even entire servers — through other countries started emerging.

In 2025, an investigative report showed that at least a billion dollars’ worth of Nvidia AI chips were allegedly funneled into China. Some sellers likened the illicit AI GPU trade to a seafood market, with brokers and retailers openly advertising the most advanced Nvidia AI chips online, from RTX 5090 GPUs to complete B200 servers.

Even with all these reports, Huang continued denying that Nvidia’s chips were being smuggled into China. “There’s no evidence of any AI chip diversion,” the then-CEO told reporters at Computex 2025. He also added, “The Grace Blackwell system is nearly two tons, and so you’re not going to be shipping — you’re not going to be putting that in your pocket or your backpack anytime soon. And so, these systems are fairly easy to keep track of... but the important thing is that the countries and the companies that we sell to recognize that diversion is not allowed, and everybody would like to continue to buy Nvidia technology, and so they very well monitor themselves very carefully and they’re quite careful about that.”

In the midst of all these AI chip smuggling rumors, the Bureau of Industry and Security, the agency in charge of enforcing U.S. trade sanctions, was found to be “inadequate” and underfunded, and it has been relying on outdated manual processes to enforce restrictions on Chinese tech companies. Despite that, companies and individuals across the world are being investigated and arrested for bypassing Washington’s export controls.

Singaporean authorities broke up a smuggling ring in early 2025 that smuggled Nvidia GPUs for Chinese AI lab DeepSeek, resulting in a raid of 22 different locations and the arrest of nine people. In October of the same year, a Singapore company called Megaspeed, which was led by Alice Huang, allegedly diverted $2 billion worth of Nvidia chips. Four Americans were then arrested the following month for shipping 400 Nvidia A100s and attempting to send 50 more Nvidia H200 GPUs to China.

An even bigger smuggling scandal unfolded some four months after the arrests, after three Super Micro employees were arrested for smuggling $2.5 billion of Nvidia hardware into China. The suspects included company co-founder Yih-Shyan “Wally” Liaw, although Super Micro itself was not implicated. The Super Micro case spanned the globe, with Taiwanese authorities raiding 12 locations related to the case in May 2026, with another nine sites and six people investigated in June. Jensen Huang urged the company to fix its export control compliance after the arrests and raids, telling the media, “We insist our partners are compliant. We hope that they will enhance and improve their regulatory compliance and prevent that from happening in the future.”

But just five months after he made this statement, one stunning accusation unraveled it all.

On October 29, 2026, The New York Times published a report that Jensen Huang may have been the target of a Chinese spy plot through the executive of a firm who was “close” to him — that executive is Alice Huang. Nvidia denied the accusation. “Megaspeed has been no different than many other regional cloud customers. Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” a company spokesperson told Tom's Hardware. “Our regional cloud customers receive ordinary business information — not sensitive NVIDIA trade secrets — and zero information relevant to national security."

But a little over a week later, U.S. intelligence sources claimed that the former Nvidia chief had an affair with the Megaspeed executive. This is the same Megaspeed that has been accused of smuggling Nvidia chips to the tune of $2 billion. By the evening of Sunday, November 8, Jensen admitted to the affair, although he did not name the third party in his statement.

Many government officials would usually turn a blind eye towards a tech executive’s personal life, but the fact that he is the leader of the U.S.’s premier AI chip maker and that he is also close to the president has caught the attention of authorities. This has led to the U.S. Congress asking him to a closed-door meeting to discuss Nvidia’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage” and the FBI investigating his relationship with the alleged Chinese spy.

The admission and allegations led to people digging deeper into Alice Huang. An investigation by The Information revealed that she was connected to a “labyrinth” of companies spanning Singapore, China, and the British Virgin Islands, and that she allegedly helped move around $7.5 billion worth of AI GPU imports. $2 billion was routed from Megaspeed to Speedmatrix, its Malaysia subsidiary, while another $4.6 billion moved directly from the Singaporean company into Shanghai Century Litong, a China-based cloud services company. It has also reportedly bought Nvidia GPUs from other sources, resulting in the estimated total value of its imports.

People close to her also said that she would leverage her relationship with Jensen to customers, often posting pictures with him on her WeChat account, showing potential clients unpublished photos with the Nvidia chief, and even boasting that he would take her to dinner and give her gifts on her birthday. It also seems that Alice Huang was not acting alone. Multiple sources say that Kang Wei, Alice Huang’s husband, was central to the operation to acquire AI GPUs. He even seemingly acknowledged her relationship with Jensen Huang and has jokingly said that she was a personal assistant to the billionaire.

However, it seems that the straw that broke the camel’s back was another allegation that Jensen had an affair with another woman — this time, with Kimberly Powell, the company’s vice president of health care who directly reported to him. Hours after this revelation, the company board announced that Jensen had resigned from all his leadership positions at Nvidia, with employees saying he looked “broken and shattered” as he left the company he spent over three decades building in disgrace. Nvidia is looking for internal and external CEO candidates. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim chief executive.

It may be the end of an era, but this does not necessarily spell the end for Jensen Huang. Despite his sudden downfall and quick removal from Nvidia, his status as the king of AI infrastructure remains valuable to the AI industry. Elon Musk even said on X that it “would be an honor” to hire him, and this could ring true for any business that wants to break into the semiconductor space. But does he even want to return?Huang's first public statement after his resignation came on X on Nov. 17, in which the former CEO said he has "outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO," and that if asked by the company's board, he would be "honored" to take the job."

Over 190,000 shareholders, partners, current and former employees, and fans, including Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, have signed an online petition to bring him back as the leader of the company. President Donald Trump called for his reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.The board, however, may not see things the same way. Multiple reports suggest that the board isn't considering bringing Huang back, and instead is focusing on a new candidate, the ongoing investigation into Huang, and preparing for national security investigations.

#10242. Find Orphan Pages and Buried Content: Build an Internal Link Graph for Your Site

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The pages of a site form a graph. Think of it as many nodes connected to each other via edges. Each of the edges are the links between the pages, and the nodes themselves are the pages. Now if you start to visualize this as a graph, you will quickly see that there are multiple ways to traverse this graph and reach a particular endpoint.

A search crawler usually uses one of these pages as a starting point and crawls through all of the pages (especially when pages are not fully covered in a sitemap).

If a page lies very deep from the home page, it means that it's not an important page. It's an indication to the search engine that this is not something that you yourself consider important, and the search engine has a lesser incentive to rank this page over pages that are linked better within your site.

The key metric here is link depth. That is the number of clicks it takes to reach a particular page from your home page.

Another key metric is broken links. If you have too many broken links, it indicates to the search engine that your site is outdated and not well maintained, and the search engine will then not want to rank your site.

The way to identify both these problems is the same: to actually walk through your site, do a crawl of your site outside in, and try to figure out what the link depth is and if there are broken links on your website.

Seed the crawl with the home page and all URLs mentioned in the sitemap. Technically, the sitemap is not required if you're doing a crawl, but going from the sitemap will allow us to detect orphaned pages as well.

Fetch the pages and follow redirects as required. Record when you're following a redirect: we do not let fetch follow it silently, we record each hop and add its target to the crawl. Record HTTP errors as they occur.

Drop URL fragments and tracking parameters. Keep trailing slashes because sometimes there can be mismatches around expectation of trailing slashes in different URLs of a site.

In order to navigate from the home page, we can use BFS. So we know that the first time we reach a page, that is the page's shortest path.

We can cut off the depth over a certain threshold, say 5, to indicate that pages beyond that are too far or too deep to be reached.

Noindex pages will still be crawled. However, we need to make a record of them being not indexed, to reflect that they will not be present in the search engine.

The desired page depth is as close to the home page as possible. Important pages should be close to the home page. The deeper you go from the home page, it signals to the search engine that the page is less important.

A couple of examples of how a deep page is present:

The suggested fix for this is that we link the pages from as close to the home page as possible:

Wasted links are those internal links that do not do the job they're supposed to do. The different types of wasted links:

We have seen here how to look at our own website through the lens of a search engine. You can use some of the code over here to create your own crawler and identify internal linking issues; the full script is in this gist. Alternatively, we can use a free internal link visualizer to identify these issues and focus only on the fixes to improve our site's internal linking.

5,000 to 6,000 gallons of propylene glycol spilled from the facility before reaching a nearby drainage ditch.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A chemical spill at a SpaceXAI facility in Southaven saw the Mississippi Department of Environmental Quality (MDEQ) get involved, with between 5,000 and 6,000 gallons of propylene glycol confirmed to have spilled. The chemical is a heat transfer fluid used in a variety of products, such as antifreeze. The chemical ultimately found its way into a drainage ditch, but MDEQ has told residents that there is no immediate threat to their health or safety, according to local news reports.

However, it has also been confirmed that water samples will be collected for further laboratory analysis while workers continue to clean the area and monitor for any further issues. It's also been reported that the chemical was contained to the drainage ditch and did not reach the nearby Horn Lake Creek.

It hasn't been a great November for SpaceXAI in Southaven. Earlier this month, it suffered a setback in a lawsuit over pollution in the area caused by its data center. The April 2026 suit targets the company's gas-powered power plant, which is used to supply power for its facility in the Greater Memphis area. It's argued that 27 gas turbines were installed without obtaining the required permits. It's also alleged that the plant could emit more than 5,000 tons of nitrogen oxides and 47 tons of formaldehyde — a cancer-causing chemical — annually. The company has tried to classify the turbines as mobile units to get around the issue, but a judge rejected that manoeuvre.

Clearly aware of the local sentiment towards its presence, SpaceXAI offered a 50% Starlink discount to anyone living near its two Memphis data centers. It also said that it would waive the hardware cost, saving customers $349. SpaceXAI said that, between late June and early September, around 2,000 residents of the greater Memphis and Southaven area had signed up for Starlink connectivity.

It was also recently announced that the City of Memphis was ready to make its first major investment as part of its new Community Benefits Ordinance, a fund that uses revenue from SpaceXAI's Colossus 1 facility. Almost $3.3 million will be pumped back into the city to help with housing and neighborhood improvements.

#10239. YouTube ‘Co-Views’ try to guess how many people are watching your TV, rolling out now

Following a change in how it counts views earlier this year, YouTube is now announcing “Co-Views” as a way for the platform to try and guess how many people are watching a YouTube video on a TV, and it counts towards the video’s total view count.

View counts on YouTube are still settling from the change earlier this year where YouTube now counts a view after the very first frame of a video plays. But, even with that, YouTube only counts a single view for each time the video is played.

Now, YouTube says it might count more views with a new “Co-Views” setup on TVs.

In a post, YouTube explains that “Co-Views” include “the estimated number of people watching together on TV” in the view count of a video. This only applies to TVs, with views from a mobile device or desktop still only ever counting as a single video. It applies to long-form videos as well as Shorts and Live videos, and only applies going forward – historical data won’t change.

YouTube says it is making this change to reflect the “over 1 billion hours” spend on YouTube through their TVs “every day” and that the added data will assist creators in channel strategy or brand pitches, while also providing a “more complete picture of how audiences are engaging with’ content, aligning with TV industry standards – including how traditional broadcast and streaming networks estimate the total number of people exposed to content.” But “Co-Views” won’t count towards earnings on the platform, as they’re not “Qualified Views.”

How is YouTube figuring out what to count here? “Guessing” is not exactly an inaccurate summarization, but Google stresses that it is not using cameras or microphones, but rather a “statistical mode.”

#10236. Nvidia says it’s ‘absurd’ to assume it predicted the agentic moment during Vera’s development – ‘product intuition’ helped drive CPU design, superior memory bandwidth will play critical role in future AI agents, CPU boss claims

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It turns out Nvidia doesn’t have a crystal ball, either.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

One criticism that has emerged of Nvidia’s Vera CPU, driven by AMD, is that Nvidia – and, to a lesser degree, Arm with its AGI CPU – couldn’t have developed an “agentic CPU,” as the chip is often characterized in Nvidia’s marketing materials. Ian Finder, the head of Nvidia’s data center CPU business, pushed back on that criticism in a recent conversation with Tom’s Hardware Premium at SC26, calling the notion that Nvidia somehow knew about the agentic wave four years ago when Vera development began "absurd."

“There's a conviction around the design philosophy of the part, right? Am I going to sit here and tell you that we have this magic crystal ball around the agentic moment? That would be absurd, right?” Finder said. “But what we do have as a company, and I think this has been proven out in our track record, is [that] we have design intuition.”

Instead of specifically designing Vera for agentic workloads, which would be impossible without a way to see several years into the future, Finder characterized the part by its codesign with Nvidia’s accelerators, something that the team has continued to work on since its first Grace data center CPU. Coming from the assumption that data centers and AI workloads are accelerated (and they largely are), Finder says the co-design approach on the CPU can start in a “conceptually simple space, like what kind of CPU do we need to keep the accelerator fed and happy?”

Although conceptually simple, Finder continually looped back to a particular word to describe aspects of Vera and, to a lesser extent, Grace. “Opinionated” is the word he used, which in the broader context of Vera’s design, shows up in two places. Grace, Finder says, introduced an opinionated memory system with Nvidia’s Scalable Coherency Fabric, while Vera extends that memory system while adding an opinionated microarchitecture, Nvidia’s Olympus core.

The picture Finder paints is one of Nvidia starting from scratch in this space, contrasting it with the “legacy constraints” of, presumably, Intel and AMD in particular, who have large pools of customers with specific needs. As we described in our original breakdown of the Olympus architecture in Vera, Nvidia’s goal doesn’t seem to be to steal market share from AMD and Intel, but rather to grab market share in a CPU space that’s vastly accelerating on the back of AI agents.

This “clean sheet of paper” approach, and Nvidia’s codesign, led to some natural assumptions about what the CPU should do. “If we have silicon that needs really, really great single-thread performance, [and] we also need concurrency, then we need to get there with IPC instead of frequency. We need to make sure we have good loaded memory latency. We need to make sure we have a lot of bandwidth, and you start to design something on a clean sheet of paper that's not encumbered by dollar-per-core economics.”

Although Finder was clear that Vera’s design didn’t explicitly center around the agents we see today, there were early indications of the workload. “To say that four years ago, when we started putting pen to paper on Vera, [that] we absolutely saw the agentic moment, would [not] be... something I would claim, but we were starting to see the beginnings of tool calling and things like that. We certainly knew what we needed from a head node, and we knew the overall shape of this problem.”

As you can see in our Vera CPU benchmarking, the chip doesn’t excel at every workload. And, from the data you can see in our recent AMD EPYC Venice benchmarking, the AMD camp has reclaimed some top spots in several benchmarks. Finder suggests that the superior memory bandwidth of Vera will play a much bigger role in the agentic future as these systems evolve to take better advantage of the compute available.

A portion of the AI industry is focused on tackling the problem of fully utilizing the GPU, Nvidia included. GPUs shouldn’t sit idle for very long (if at all) with the demand today, and they don’t. The challenge today is spinning up these agent sandboxes quickly, within milliseconds, so that the CPU isn’t sitting idle, either.

Finder provided an example of using a coding agent. If you’ve used a coding agent before, you’re probably familiar with this process. After prompting, the model will make several small tool calls to read and locate files, passing back to the model in between. These are tiny calls, almost negligible, and the vast majority of the time is spent in the model on the GPU. Then, it may start compiling and testing the project, which is much more bound by the CPU. The GPU, Finder says, isn't sitting idle during that time. It’s being leveraged for another purpose. Finder suggests “that very same thing is going to happen on the CPU.”

This “very interesting computer science problem,” as Finder described it, is being investigated by AI startups such as Daytona and E2B to spin up and close down agentic sandboxes in milliseconds. And serving more agents will naturally put pressure on the memory system, which is an area where Vera has an edge. “We built a product that does really, really well without such optimizations, but as a design point that really will lean into them when the industry starts to catch up to this,” Finder said.

Vera is a fascinating chip, regardless of what you think of it and how it’s presented in marketing materials (from Nvidia itself or elsewhere). Although Grace was the starting point internally, Vera has ridden the wave of agentic to become a much bigger player in the world of CPUs, and Nvidia has already announced that it will follow up with its Rosa CPU featuring the new Rigel microarchitecture. Vera is an opinionated CPU indeed, and time will tell if that opinion is correct.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10233. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

It's not the first time eminent domain fears have been raised around AI data centers

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While Ohioans have rejected the building of new data centers at every turn, they might not have much choice in the future. That's if a newly leaked bill that could allow data centers to be classified as public utilities is allowed to progress. The move would see the list of who can qualify for eminent domain law expanded, moving beyond entities owned or operated by municipal corporations, electric cooperatives, and agencies holding a certificate of public convenience and necessity granted by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission. The bill, leaked to WCPO Cincinnati, would add "and an agency holding a certificate of environmental compatibility and public need from the power siting board for constructing an electric transmission line and associated facilities or gas pipeline and its associated facilities."

Legal experts have warned that the change could mean a transmission developer, like a power company, would be able to seize land to be used for a data center or a facility that would power one. The writing has been on the wall for a while, too, after it was discovered in June that the Ohio Business Roundtable trade group recommended that lawmakers change eminent domain law to "extend possession authority to energy infrastructure projects once public use and necessity have been established.”

This follows a July 2026 report that the government was able to seize private land for new transmission lines needed to power new data centers. The law gives the government the power to take private land without the need for the owner's consent, so long as it is for public use and the owner receives appropriate compensation. The government can also delegate the same land-grabbing power to “private entities or common carriers,” which includes utility companies.

This is already happening, too. Earlier this year, the largest utility company in Georgia required the removal of 30 residential properties in order to upgrade its grid. Residents claimed that the expansion was to power AI data centers, a fact given more weight by the fact that one such data center was located just a few miles away.

Despite this, eminent domain law can still be used for good. Nashville set out to use the same law to block the building of a 69,220-square-foot single-story data center next to a zoo. Nashville Metro Council approved the use of eminent domain as a way to buy the land from a local developer who planned the $700 million data center buildout.

#10225. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.
