#10221. Cost per task: Using cheap classifiers to route across Gemini models and thinking levels

Per-token API prices keep falling, yet engineering bills for autonomous coding agents keep climbing. Price per million tokens isn't the metric that actually governs production economics anymore. The dominating metric is now cost per successful task: total spend across every loop iteration, tool call, retry and internal thinking pass divided by the number of tasks that pass verification.

When an agent calls a high-thinking reasoning configuration for every micro-decision in its harness, you're paying deep-reasoning prices for questions a lightweight configuration already answers accurately. To prevent this unnecessary overhead, consider adding a lightweight routing layer that can intercept routine decisions before they ever reach an expensive reasoning model.That is the premise behind wiring TypeSafe's Jev classifier in front of Google Antigravity's model calls. Rather than generating text token by token, Jev—a specialized structured-output classifier designed for high-throughput decision pipelines—evaluates structured classification primitives in a single forward pass: a Choice from a list, a numeric Score or a Noul, which returns a 0 to 1 probability for a binary statement.

For full disclosure, Google has no partnership with TypeSafe, and this article is not an endorsement of TypeSafe or its products. Jev is used here as one example of a lightweight classifier, and the same routing pattern can be built with other classifiers, including Google-native ones. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab, for example, runs the same kind of classifier on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, in your own Google Cloud project.

We ran a 50-question, auto-graded, coding-agent benchmark against live Gemini and Jev APIs to test whether a near-free classifier can route each task to the cheapest model that still gets it right. Here's what we learned:

The naive way to ask Jev to route a coding task is a single broad Choice question: simple or complex. When we tested that first on 16 coding-agent tasks, Jev routed nearly everything to simple, even tasks requiring an LRU cache trace or modular exponentiation. TypeSafe's documentation warns against this exact anti-pattern. If a question weighs multiple factors or requires a meta-judgment, decompose it into an atomic check.

When we replaced the broad Choice question with one atomic Noul question, it fixed the separation immediately. The question was: does answering this require performing more than one sequential calculation, simulation step or logical deduction, rather than a single direct lookup, format check or one-step classification?

On those initial 16 tasks, Jev routed 13 of 16 in agreement with our hand-labeled difficulty on the first run and 14 of 16 on the second pass. Many engineering teams ask whether they even need a classifier API when they can write a zero-cost regex and prompt-length heuristic in Python, matching words like sequence, modulo and race condition or escalating any prompt over 35 words.

On the 16 development questions used to write those exact rules, the local heuristic scored 15 of 16, or 93.8%. To test whether that heuristic survived contact with realistic coding-agent traffic, we froze its code without changing a character and added 34 unseen held-out tasks across seven coding-agent buckets: fact extraction, schema validation, short classification, single-hop code checks, state simulation traces, math or logic chains and subtle language-semantics bugs.

On the 34 held-out questions, the frozen heuristic dropped 23.2 percentage points to 70.6%. Realistic simple tasks such as extracting a port from a Kubernetes YAML manifest, a file path from a unified git diff header or a line number from a Python traceback exceeded 35 words and triggered false escalations. Conversely, concise multi-step code traces such as mutable default arguments across calls, Python dictionary key hashing on {1: "a", True: "b", 1.0: "c"} and C short-circuit evaluation lacked the heuristic's hardcoded puzzle words and were falsely routed to the simple path. Jev classified all 34 unseen tasks with 100.0% agreement, 34 of 34, reaching 48 of 50, or 96.0%, across the full suite.

A common mistake when building a fast-path router is assuming that setting a low maxOutputTokens cap on a reasoning model will make it spend less on easy tasks. Our 50-question data shows why that assumption fails.

When we compared gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=300 against gemini-3.8-flash with maxOutputTokens=4096 while leaving thinkingConfig at the API default, both configurations spent almost identical thinking tokens on the 27 tasks Jev labeled SIMPLE:

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

Capping the model's output at 300 tokens didn't save any money on easy tasks. The cap is only a ceiling, and easy answers never came close to it. On the easy tasks, the capped version actually cost slightly more than the uncapped one. All the cap did was break 7 of the 25 hard tasks. On six of them, the model ran out of room while still reasoning and never gave an answer. On the seventh, it squeezed its reasoning to fit, finished on time and got the answer wrong.

So a Jev router that sends easy tasks to the capped model and hard ones to normal Flash got all 50 right. It still cost slightly more than skipping the router and using normal Flash for everything, and it was slower. If your cheap option doesn't actually make the model think less on easy tasks, no router can save you money. Not even a perfect one that costs nothing.

The obvious way to spend less on thinking is to ask the model to think less. So we ran a second round of tests with two lighter options: Flash-Lite, which doesn't think at all by default, and Gemini 3.8 Flash with its thinking level set to low. We added those results to the same results file, matched question by question.

A note on timing. Most of the router's time per task is spent in Flash-Lite and Flash calls, which we measured in the same run and time window as the plain Flash baseline, so the speed comparison between them is fair. Jev's own decision is a small share of the total and comes from the first round. Speed comparisons against default Flash or Pro mix the two rounds, so read those as approximate.

Note: Figures reflect single-run experimental measurements on a sample test suite; actual latencies, token counts, and billed costs will vary based on prompt length, context caching, and production load.

The 7-bucket breakdown shows where gemini-3.5-flash-lite and the heuristic router missed tasks and how Router Jev: Flash-Lite to 3.8 LOW recovered 100% accuracy:

The strongest setup without a router was Gemini 3.8 Flash on low thinking. It solved all 50 tasks. That's the number a router had to beat.

The Jev router beat it. It sends every task to Flash-Lite first and escalates only what Jev flags as complex. It also solved all 50 tasks, and it can help reduce both cost and response time compared with running low thinking on everything. On prompts this short the cost difference per task is small, so for an interactive agent the shorter wait is the bigger benefit.

Why it works. On its own, Flash-Lite answered every simple task in half a second but missed 6 of the 25 hard ones. Jev flagged all 6 as complex with high confidence and escalated them. That brought its cost close to what a perfect router would achieve.

The keyword heuristic was cheaper, but only because it got things wrong. It sent 5 hard tasks to Flash-Lite, and 2 of them failed, for 48 of 50.

Escalating to a heavier setting didn't pay here. Routing hard tasks to Flash on default thinking was cheaper than running default thinking everywhere. It still cost more than running low thinking on everything, which already solved all 50. A heavier escalation target only makes sense when the low setting starts failing.

Limits: one run per setup, 50 prompts with hand-assigned difficulty and simple automatic grading.

Two things in our results only show up once you look past it.

Routing and caching pull in opposite directions. Our test prompts were short, standalone tasks, and on those the Jev router helped reduce both cost and response time. Real coding agents often look different. They send the same big block of context on every turn, such as a map of the repository and the tool definitions. Agent Platform Context Caching stores that block so you pay 75% to 90% less to resend it. The catch is that a cache belongs to one model. If your agent switches between Flash-Lite and Flash from turn to turn, it either keeps two caches or loses the discount. The practical split: route short, self-contained tasks like log parsing or schema checks, and keep long, context-heavy turns on one model.

Match the model to the workload. Gemini 3.1 Pro is built for deep, multi-step reasoning, and it brings that same depth to every request, including short lookups that don't need it. For a workload made mostly of quick checks and extractions, a Flash configuration is the better fit, and Pro is best saved for the tasks that need its reasoning depth. Model configurations can also apply different safety settings to security-related prompts. In our run, the Pro configuration declined two short code-security review prompts that Flash answered. If your agent performs security reviews, test those prompts on the configuration you plan to use.

Other ways to build the router. Jev is one way to get a fast classifier, but not the only one. The Getting started with discriminative models codelab runs the same Choice, Score and Noul questions on DiffusionGemma, Google DeepMind's open-weights model, hosted on a Compute Engine GPU VM or on Cloud Run in your own Google Cloud project. An open-source server exposes the same API in front of it, so the routing code in this article only needs a different endpoint. Our results come from Jev, so measure accuracy and latency on your own tasks before switching, and keep in mind that a self-hosted model is billed by the GPU-hour rather than by the token.

Taken together, the results give three practical rules.

Start with Flash on low thinking before you build a router. It solved all 50 tasks and cost considerably less than the default setting. Lowering the output token limit is not a substitute, because it doesn't reduce how much the model thinks on easy tasks. Set the thinking level directly, with thinkingLevel: "LOW".

Use Jev to route short, standalone tasks. Send simple tasks to Flash-Lite and hard ones to Flash on low thinking. Ask Jev one simple yes-or-no question per factor. In our test, this helped reduce cost and response time compared with running Flash on low thinking for everything, and came close to what a perfect router would achieve. Keep long, context-heavy turns on a single model so caching keeps working. For high-volume warehouse workloads, Jeff Nelson's BigQuery and Cloud Run cascade pattern applies the same idea.

Measure what your agent actually spends. Send your agent's traces and thinking-token counts to Cloud Logging and BigQuery, so you can see which tasks drive your spend and catch truncation or retries that inflate it. The benchmark code will be published after open-source review.

In summary, the most cost-effective way to run an agent is to match each task to the model that fits it. Start with a fast model on a light setting. You'll often find it already gets the job done. Then add a small, almost free classifier like Jev to send only the hard tasks to a stronger model, and ask simple yes-or-no questions rather than one broad one. Judge every option by what it costs per task done right, not per token.

#10219. Alyah ⭐️: Toward Robust Evaluation of Emirati Dialect Capabilities in Arabic LLMs

#10216. Philippine Migrant Worker Data Likely Leaked in 69GB Government Breach

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

An unknown operator appears to have exfiltrated SQL Server backups from the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration in under a half hour.

An unknown threat actor managed to gain access to sensitive databases belonging to the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration, exfiltrating nearly 69GB of information on workers, contracts, and financial records, according to an analysis of a staging server used in the breach.

Over 24 minutes, the attacker copied 27 backup databases with information on overseas-employment case management, including accreditation, contracts, and cashier and LandBank records, according to threat intelligence firm Hunt.io's Oct. 28 analysis of the logs found on a Singaporean staging server used by the attacker. The cyberattackers used privileged escalation tools, namely GodPotato and SeImpersonate, and the Impacket network library for Python to move the data, but the trail stopped at the staging server, says Esteban Borges, head of research for Hunt.io.

The server had been accessed later, but by whom is not clear, Borges says. While Hunt.io has not yet discovered the data as advertised for sale, the information may have been detected and removed, but that seems unlikely, he says.

Related:State Bank of India Suffers a Customer PII Data Leak

"I won't claim the backups stopped on the staging server," he says, so it's "still a breach, since the data was copied out of the agency’s control and the log shows the transfer completing."

This is not the first time that cybersecurity firms have found evidence that Philippine government data had been stolen. In August, Hunt.io discovered another threat actor's workspace hosting a number of tools and, in that case, 1.2GB of data stolen from a Philippine nuclear regulatory agency and a maritime shipbuilding contractor. A year ago, Philippine police arrested the mayor of a small town after discovering she was a Chinese national posing as a Filipina citizen and operating a cyber-scam center.

Whether the current incident involved China or cybercriminal groups is not clear. While Hunt.io did find some Chinese language fragments in the parts of the code, that code belonged to a third-party software library; thus, Hunt.io did not attribute the hack. The company did say, however, that both cybercriminals and nation-state adversaries would find value in the personally identifiable information (PII).

"That kind of migrant-worker PII, plus contract and payment data, is useful for fraud and identity theft, so it has commercial value," Borges says. "It's also a detailed map of a diaspora population, which can interest a state actor."

The latest insight into attacks on the Philippine government came after Hunt.io researchers discovered an open directory on a server hosted on the Singaporean address space of a Helsinki-based firm, UpCloud Ltd., the analysis stated. The open directory contained tools and log files for the attack, including a rebranded version of the GodPotato privilege escalation library, a PowerShell stager to unpack tools in memory, and the full Impacket Python environment.

Related:Russian Influence Operation Widely Targets Latin America

While Hunt.io considered the incident a confirmed compromise, the lack of runtime telemetry meant that Borges could not answer where the attacker moved the data.

"[W]e still can’t answer ... how they got in, whether the payload ran, and where the data went," he says.

However, the tools and the attacker's process indicated an operator with some strong ability, but who also made some simple mistakes, Borges says.

"The payload engineering is competent, [but] the operational security is poor — they left the staging server and its logs wide open," he says. "[It was] someone who can build the tool properly but was careless about keeping their own server quiet. I wouldn't call that top-tier or amateur."

The impact of the breach may boil down to the identity of the attacker and what they do with the stolen data. While Hunt.io notified the CERT Philippines (CERT-PH) and the Department of Migrant Workers (DMW), the company declined to provide any more details of the national responses to the leak.

Related:Venezuelan Cartel's Malware Honcho Nabbed for ATM Jackpotting

However, Borges did say that, so far, the information has not appeared for sale on the Dark Web.

"We haven't seen the backups surface for sale or in a leak," he says. "We never opened the backups themselves."

Governments in the region need to scan their own networks and lock down their attack surface area, he says. Workers with too many privileges allow attackers with tools such as SeImpersonate to gain more access to assets on the network. Hunt.io also found that the migrant-worker portal exposed a number of risky ports, including SMB on 445 and WinRM on 5985 and 47001.

#10214. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

#10210. Logging AI Prompts and Responses in Java Without Leaking Sensitive Data

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picture this. A customer writes to your support chatbot: "Hi, I'm Asha, my card ending 4111 was charged twice, call me on 98400 12345." Your Java service wraps that message in a prompt, sends it to a model, and logs the whole thing so you can debug it later. Congratulations, you now have a customer's name, phone number and card details sitting in a plain text log file that half the engineering team can open.

This happens more often than people admit. Logging AI calls is genuinely useful, but prompts and responses are some of the most personal text your application ever touches. Here's how to keep the usefulness and drop the risk.

A regular web request is easy to log: URL, status code, time taken. AI calls are messier.

The output changes from run to run. If a user says "your bot gave me a wrong answer yesterday", the only proof of what happened is what you sent and what came back at that moment. You can't just replay it and expect the same result.

Prompts are also built, not typed. The final text is usually a template, a few retrieved document chunks, some chat history and the user's message stitched together. When something looks off, the bug could be in any of those pieces, and only the assembled prompt shows you which one.

Then there's cost. You pay by the token, so without per-call records you can't say which feature is burning money. And quality drifts over time as models get upgraded and templates get edited. Old logs are your only baseline.

So yes, you need these logs. The real question is what goes into them.

Before writing any masking code, agree on what counts as sensitive. In most business apps that means:

Names, emails, phone numbers and home addresses

Card numbers, bank details and government ID numbers

Health, legal or job-related details people mention in free text

Internal material like contract wording, pricing rules or source code

The first three groups follow patterns, so they're easy to catch. The last two don't, and that's where most teams get caught out. Keep that in mind, because it shapes the design below.

The safest sensitive value is the one that never reaches the log. So split what you record into two buckets.

The first is metadata: a request ID, a hashed user or tenant ID, model name, prompt template name and version, token counts, latency, retry count, error type and estimated cost. None of this is personal, and it answers most day-to-day questions.

The second is content, meaning the actual prompt and response text. You only need this when you're investigating quality, and even then you rarely need all of it. A sensible default is to log metadata for every call and masked content for a small sample, maybe five percent, plus every failed call.

Here's a mistake that's easy to make: letting the log appender clean things up afterwards. By then the raw text may already be in a buffer, an async queue or a crash dump. Mask first, then log.

The cleanest way is one small component that every AI log call has to pass through:

public interface PiiMasker {     String mask(String input); }

Nothing else is allowed to write raw prompts. One chokepoint is much easier to review and test than masking sprinkled across fifty classes.

For structured data like emails, cards and tokens, regular expressions do the job well:

public class RegexPiiMasker implements PiiMasker {     private static final Map RULES = new LinkedHashMap<>();     static {         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("[A-Za-z0-9.%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}"), "[EMAIL]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b"), "[CARD]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\+?\d{1,3}[ -]?)?\d{10}\b"), "[PHONE]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("(?i)bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9.~+/=-]+"), "Bearer [TOKEN]");     }     @Override     public String mask(String input) {         if (input == null || input.isBlank()) return input;         String result = input;         for (var rule : RULES.entrySet()) {             result = rule.getKey().matcher(result).replaceAll(rule.getValue());         }         return result;     } }

A few small choices matter here. The patterns are compiled once, not on every call. The order is fixed, because a card number can otherwise get half eaten by the phone rule. And the replacement labels such as [EMAIL] keep the sentence readable, so whoever debugs it later can still follow what the user was asking.

Regex won't catch a name like "Asha Raman" or an address buried mid-sentence. For those you have a few options, and they work best together.

You can run a named-entity recognition model that tags people, places and organisations, then mask anything above a confidence threshold. You can do exact replacement of values you already know, such as the customer's name from the current session. Or, best of all, you can mask at the field level while building the prompt. If your code knows that customer.getEmail() is going into the template, swap in a placeholder right there instead of trying to spot it afterwards. Knowing beats guessing every time.

Plain redaction can remove too much. If the same email shows up five times in a conversation, five identical [EMAIL] tags hide the fact that it was one person.

The fix is consistent pseudonyms. Turn each real value into a short token using a keyed hash, so the same email always becomes something like EMAIL_7f3a. You can still follow the thread, but nobody can read the original from the log. Use an HMAC with a secret from your secrets manager. A plain unkeyed hash of a phone number can be brute-forced in minutes, because there just aren't that many phone numbers.

One warning: if you also store a lookup table to reverse the tokens, that table is now sensitive data too. Most teams are better off with one-way tokens.

Free-text log lines are hard to search and easy to leak through. Write one JSON record per AI call with clearly separate fields:

log.info("ai_call",     kv("requestId", requestId),     kv("model", model),     kv("promptVersion", "support-reply-v4"),     kv("inputTokens", usage.inputTokens()),     kv("outputTokens", usage.outputTokens()),     kv("latencyMs", elapsed),     kv("promptMasked", masker.mask(prompt)),     kv("responseMasked", masker.mask(response)));

Now dashboards for cost and latency never need to touch the text fields at all. You can also keep the two kinds of data for different lengths of time: metadata for a year, masked content for a couple of weeks. Put the request ID into your logging framework's MDC so every line belonging to one call, including retries and tool calls, carries the same ID.

Streaming trips people up. Tokens arrive in tiny pieces, and a card number can be split across two chunks, which means masking each chunk on its own will miss it. Collect the full response in a buffer, mask it once the stream ends, and log that. If the stream dies halfway, run the partial buffer through the same masker.

Tool calls are the other trap. When the model calls one of your Java methods, the arguments and return values are often the most sensitive thing in the whole exchange: order details, account balances, addresses. Treat them as part of the prompt and response. Log the tool name, duration and outcome by default, and log arguments only after masking.

Masking lowers the risk but never removes it, so surround it with some ordinary discipline:

Give content fields a short retention period. Old prompt archives are liabilities.

Limit who can read AI logs. Debugging a model doesn't mean someone needs to read customer messages.

Encrypt log storage and the pipeline that feeds it.

Log the hashed user ID so you can find and delete one person's records when they ask.

Allow verbose logging in development with fake data, and enforce strict settings in production through configuration, not through people remembering.

A masker that quietly stops working is worse than having none, because everyone assumes the logs are clean. Write tests for it:

@Test void masksEmailAndCardNumber() {     String raw = "Contact me at asha@example.com, card 4111 1111 1111 1111";     String safe = new RegexPiiMasker().mask(raw);     assertFalse(safe.contains("asha@example.com"));     assertFalse(safe.contains("4111"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[EMAIL]"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[CARD]")); }

Build a small set of messy, realistic samples: numbers with odd spacing, mixed languages, addresses inside sentences, text pasted from spreadsheets. Run them in your CI pipeline, and add a new case every time a leak turns up. A scheduled job that scans stored logs for patterns that should never appear makes a good safety net too.

A few I'd watch for. Logging raw text at debug level and assuming production never enables it; debug flags get switched on during incidents, so mask at every level. Masking only the user's message while forgetting retrieved documents, system prompts and tool results. Letting SDK exceptions carry the full request body into the logs. Leaving wire-level HTTP logging on in production, which bypasses your masker completely. And treating masking as finished once it ships, when every new prompt template can bring new kinds of data.

Every AI call writes a structured metadata record with a request ID

All prompt and response text goes through a single masker first

Streams are masked after assembly, and tool arguments are masked too

Content logging is sampled, short-lived and access-controlled

Tests cover the masker, with a regression case for every past leak

Client libraries aren't dumping raw payloads into the logs

Good logs turn an AI feature from a black box into something you can actually debug, price and improve. The same logs can also expose the people who trusted you with their details. The way through is fairly plain: record metadata for every call, push all content through one well-tested masker, use pseudonyms when you need to follow a conversation, handle streams and tools carefully, and keep retention short. Set that up before launch, not after the first incident, and you get visibility without regret.

#10205. Incidents Move Too Fast for Pen & Paper

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

Organizations may not be ready for completely autonomous communication processes, but there are places where automating information updates would alleviate pressure.

Organizations steadily deploy automated threat detection and response processes; some even take it a step further with fully artificial intelligence (AI) security operations centers (SOCs). Automation can catch an incident in seconds, yet communication remains mostly manual across an industry that requires more transparency.

The disconnect can strain relationships and trust as customers wait for incident impact and mitigation answers. Hands-on communications also put more pressure on chief information security officers (CISOs), who must relay timely updates to IT personnel and executives as the incident clock winds down.

Incident response (IR) communications are increasingly too much for humans to coordinate without more help. But disclosures contain sensitive information — the status of a company's security posture or how they responded when alerted to an incident — and automation takes away some of the control leadership wants to hold onto in regard to timing and specifics.

Related:As Test Takers Turn to AI, Exam Security Plays Catch-Up

From a coordination and information standpoint, automating communications is absolutely important, says Tim Brown, chief strategy officer at Cytactic. That could mean implementing a centralized place to view everyone's status versus relying on meetings to receive updates.

A dedicated crisis communication platform could help, Brown says. A single click of a button could notify everyone, and that includes contingency options if normal communications go down, which is typical following an incident where systems go offline.

Brown is no stranger to hectic IR communication scenarios. While trying to manage the SolarWinds attack fallout, the former CISO got pulled into one crisis after the other. He oversaw five different teams, coordinated tasks, scheduled meetings, received updates from everyone, and tried to keep the customers briefed.

As he looks back now, he appreciates how much help everyone around him was. Still, some preparation was missing: more tabletop exercises, meeting the appropriate people beforehand, and technology to ease communications.

IR communications are complicated. Organizations may wait to disclose certain information, so they do not tip off attackers. Sometimes victim organizations post customer-only private advisories, but that leaves public awareness and downstream victims vulnerable. Yet other times, companies simply choose not to disclose or downplay effects to save face.

The Securities and Exchange Commission and Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) continue to update and adopt new disclosure regulations in light of more disruptive attacks. The Cyber Incident Reporting for Critical Infrastructure Act (CIRCIA) of 2022, after missing several earlier deadlines, is nearing finalization. Once in effect, the rule would require covered entities to report substantial cyber incidents to CISA within 72 hours of discovery, and critical infrastructure organizations that make ransom payments would need to report within 24 hours.

Related:NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure

Even if organizations want to disclose any information in good faith, that poses challenges. A wide net of victims, third parties, and supply chain risks abound. It's increasingly more difficult to even know who is affected these days.

Douglas McKee, director of vulnerability intelligence at Rapid7, ventures to guess that most security leaders would cite communication problems, and they usually exist long before an incident ever happens. For example, security teams tend to talk in terms of common vulnerability exposures (CVEs) and indicators of compromise, while the people making business decisions need to know what any of that actually means.

On a normal day, that gap is annoying, but during an incident, the language barrier gets amplified because everyone needs answers at the same moment, even though the facts change by the hour. And nobody wants to be the person who said the wrong thing publicly, McKee adds.

Related:Behind the Scenes of How Operation Blackout Went Down

"Third parties make this even harder," he tells Dark Reading. "I've seen plenty of situations where a vendor knows about a problem in their product well before their customers do, but the notification lands in a generic inbox or goes to a contact who left the company two years ago.”

Attackers benefit from that confusion, he notes, adding how important it is for defenders to agree internally on what's happening so they can relay information to customers confidently.

Organizations want to be more transparent but continually struggle with how to personalize communication outreach, says Brian Killeen, vice president of global identity solutions at TransUnion. Whether in day-to-day fraud communications or during an active incident, the more they can leverage AI, insights, and understanding the better they can adapt, he adds .

Right now, it's easier to automate detection and response because the inputs can be fairly structured, explains McKee; a log entry either matches a rule or it doesn't. IR communication, on the other hand, involves judgement calls with legal and reputational consequences.

"Organizations are understandably nervous about letting a machine decide what goes out the door with their name on it," McKee says. "Let's face it: An analyst can shrug off a false positive in a few minutes, but there's no easy way to 'unsend' an inaccurate breach notice to your customers."

That doesn't mean automating communication is off the table — it just needs to be pointed at the right parts of the problem, he says. Organizations can automate pulling together the incident timeline or tracking which stakeholders have been notified and when. McKee also recommends using tools to draft a first version of an update from data the responders already have.

It's all about freeing people up to spend their time on decisions that actually need a human. The way McKee looks at it, "automation should get the draft in front of the right person faster, and that person should still be the one who verifies and hits send."

Arielle spent the last decade working as a reporter, transitioning from human interest stories to covering all things cybersecurity related in 2020. Now, as a features writer for Dark Reading, she delves into the security problems enterprises face daily, providing context and actionable steps. She looks for stories that go past the initial news to understand where the industry is going. Her coverage areas include identity and access management, cyber risk and operations, industrial control systems, operational technology, and ransomware trends.

#10204. Agent Distraction

What is easy for humans to skim past in code, agents see as a call to action. They do not know better, latching on to that which is littered across the files read during the planning and research phase of the coding journey.

As agents' agency through tools and skills grows the ability for side-quests scales proportionally. Obsolete specs, long resolved bugs, and lagging documentation no longer rest in long forgotten corners of the codebase, but rather are unintentionally ingested as context indirecting and misdirecting the next wave of coders - the agents. This indirection is not free: in the best case it only costs tokens, steps, and time - in the worst case it distracts and grounds the agent on falsehoods leading to incorrect logic and decisions during implementation.

Measuring how often agents fall into these traps across millions of monthly sessions is impossible with manual human review - we need an objective signal derivable from the code itself. Inline TODO(bug/...) comments provide the perfect proxy. TODOs tend to be left behind long after the underlying bug is resolved or abandoned, while the linked bug ticket acts as the canonical record. By cross-referencing every TODO an agent encounters against the TODO's bug status and the user's prompt, we can automatically separate valid work from wasted detours at scale.

By leveraging the distributed traces of agent interactions across thousands of engineers and over hundreds of millions of individual agent steps, we quantify the impact of TODO distractions in the codebase and how agents act on them:

Controlling for developer, codebase complexity, task type, model tier, number of files read, initial prompt length, and the step where the bug reference appeared, using a multi-way fixed-effects regression with developer-clustered standard errors, we see that unprompted bug detours add substantial overhead (all stat sig p<0.0001). Per session:

The Obsolete Detour: In the following trajectory we can observe how this drift plays out. During an investigation into an unrelated bug The agent views SurfaceControlTest.java and sees // TODO(bug/XXX)

This bug, while fixed in 2023, remains lingering in the codebase leading to subsequent investigation distraction across Steps with the agent digging into the bug to learn more.

The Eager Agent: In the trajectory the agent encountered a TODO in the bug it was investigating and decided (unprompted) to "fix" the code

The agent executed multiple Code Search and internal knowledge search queries for the bug ultimately resulting in the agent changing the ModelDownloaderV1 code. Later in the trajectory the human challenged the scope creep.

With the model when admitting it was an incorrect increase in scope:

While in both of these cases they were only trivial to minor detours - they were real distractions costing time, tokens, and human intervention.

As part of addressing this finding at scale we created agents that scrubbed the totality of our codebases, finding bug references in the code that were marked as fixed or obsolete for cleanup. That step alone however was not enough to determine if the TODOs in the codebase were load-bearing or not. The agents had to go further looking at the history of the bug, the file, and changes to the codebase, at times spanning years of history. Only in cases where the agent was able to confirm that the TODO and the intent behind the TODO was obsolete did our agent send a change for review to delete it from the codebase. As a result of this multi-layer approach, our todo cleaner has been able to remove thousands of actually obsolete references over the last 6 months exceeding 95% acceptance from human reviewers.

In recap, as we task agents on more and more ambitious problems, "agent friendly codebases" require more than just the code in the hotpath being healthy. The forgotten corners of the codebase now more than ever carry a significant tax levied against agents.

Tooling like presubmit analyzers, entire corpus scanners, and automated coding agents to remediate dead-todos offer out of the box ready to scale solutions to address the tax enabling a healthier and distraction free codebase for agents to thrive.

Projects that once were on the backburner to cleanup lingering debt but deprioritized due to perceived low value need to be reevaluated with the new agents acting at scale on your codebase.

#10202. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.

#10197. Securing FastAPI Applications: JWT Authentication and Rate Limiting with Redis

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a modern web API is only half the battle. Ensuring it is secure, resilient, and capable of handling malicious traffic is what separates a hobby project from a production-ready service. When building highly scalable applications, we typically lean on stateless architectures. However, features like token revocation and rate limiting require state.

In this guide, we will explore the architectural concepts behind building a secure API using FastAPI, Redis, JSON Web Tokens (JWT), and SlowAPI. We will break down the theory, the tech stack, and the flow of data—without getting bogged down in the code.

Before diving into the architecture, it is crucial to understand the role each tool plays in our security model:

Security starts with how you manage your secrets. The first step in our architectural journey is setting up a configuration file and a local environment file.

Hardcoding secret keys, database URLs, or algorithm types directly into your application is a severe security risk. Instead, we load these from environment variables. The application uses a settings manager to read variables like the Redis host, port, the JWT secret key, the algorithm (typically HS256), and the token expiration time. This ensures that sensitive data stays out of your version control system.

To handle rate limiting and token tracking, we need a connection to Redis. Because FastAPI is inherently asynchronous, we initialize an asynchronous Redis client.

Redis acts as the central state-holder for our API. By using Redis, we ensure that even if our API is running on five different servers behind a load balancer, the rate limits and the token blacklists remain globally synchronized.

Next, we establish the utility functions that handle the cryptographic heavy lifting. This step is divided into two main parts:

Here, we initialize the FastAPI application and attach slowapi.

slowapi needs a way to identify the client making the request. By default, it uses the client's IP address. We attach the limiter to the application state and register an exception handler. This ensures that if a user breaches the rate limit, the API gracefully returns a "429 Too Many Requests" error instead of crashing.

We also set up a dependency that acts as a security guard for our protected routes. This dependency intercepts incoming requests, extracts the token, and decodes it. It then checks Redis to see if the token has been blacklisted (for example, if the user logged out before the token expired). If the token is valid and not blacklisted, the request proceeds; otherwise, a "401 Unauthorized" error is thrown.

The login endpoint is where the magic happens. When a user submits their credentials, the API verifies the username and password. In a production scenario, this involves querying a database; in our example, we verify it against a hardcoded credential.

Once the credentials are validated, the API generates a JWT. To ensure we can invalidate this token later if needed, we store the token in Redis with a time-to-live (TTL) that matches the token's expiration time. Finally, the token is returned to the client, which will store it and send it in the Authorization header of future requests.

Finally, we create a route that requires both authentication and rate limiting.

Before the route's logic even executes, two things happen:

If the client passes both security checks, they are granted access to the highly secure data provided by the route.

To validate this system, you would conceptually walk through several test scenarios. First, you test the login flow with correct and incorrect credentials to ensure tokens are only issued to valid users.

Next, you test the rate limit by rapidly firing requests at the protected route to ensure the "429 Too Many Requests" response triggers. Finally, you test the security guard by trying to access the route with no token, an expired token, and a manually blacklisted token to ensure the API correctly denies access in all scenarios.

Building a secure API requires layering defenses. By combining FastAPI's speed, Redis's fast state management, JWT's stateless authentication, and slowapi's rate-limiting capabilities, you create a system that is both robust and scalable.

While this foundational text explains the core concepts, moving to production requires adding token refresh flows, implementing per-user rate limiting (rather than just IP-based), deploying Redis with high availability, and ensuring your secrets are managed by a secure vault. These practices will ensure your API remains secure as your user base grows.
